scale.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Where the cancellation must occur]
\label{rem:cross-lag}
The trivial bound is $\sum_hh|C^{[v]}_h|\le\sum_hh(v-h)\le v^3/6$.
RH requires the signed weighted sum to be $v^{2+o(1)}$. Absolute values
cannot give this at the natural scale: even square-root cancellation
$|C^{[v]}_h|\ll v^{1/2+o(1)}$ in every single lag would give only
$\sum_hh|C^{[v]}_h|\ll v^{5/2+o(1)}$. Corollary~\CGref{cor:RH-large-lags}
sharpens the location of the unresolved cancellation: at the $v^2$ scale,
only lags $h>\sqrt v$ remain. By Proposition~\CGref{prop:weighted-global} the
signed weighted identity itself decomposes excursion by excursion.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Lag localization of the energy of an excursion}

\begin{proposition}[Energy beyond a lag cutoff]
\label{prop:lag-localization}
For every excursion $I_\nu$ of length $\ell=\ell_\nu$ and every
$1\le h_0<\ell$,
\begin{equation}
\Bigl|Q_\nu+\sum_{h_0<h<\ell}h\,C^{(\nu)}_h\Bigr|
\le h_0\sum_{h\le h_0}\bigl|C^{(\nu)}_h\bigr| .
\end{equation}
Consequently, if $\sum_{h\le h_0}|C^{(\nu)}_h|\le\delta h_0\ell$, then
\begin{equation}
\sum_{h_0<h<\ell}h\,C^{(\nu)}_h
=-Q_\nu+O(\delta h_0^2\ell).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Split $Q_\nu=-\sum_{h<\ell}hC^{(\nu)}_h$ at $h_0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Averaged Chowla on linear-scale intervals]
\label{cor:chowla-linear}
Let $(u_j,u_j+L_j]$ be intervals with $L_j\to\infty$ and $u_j\le BL_j$ for a
fixed $B$, and let $h_j\to\infty$ with $h_j=o(L_j)$. Then
\begin{equation}
\sum_{h\le h_j}\bigl|C^{(u_j,u_j+L_j]}_h\bigr|=o(h_jL_j).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Put $X_j=L_j-h_j$. Then $X_j\sim L_j$ and, for all large $j$,
$u_j\le2B X_j$. Apply the shifted form of
\cite[Theorem~1.6]{MRT15}, whose hypotheses explicitly allow translation
parameters $b_r\le A X_j$, to the two-point M\"obius correlation. The same
paper states immediately after Theorem~1.6 that the argument proving its
averaged Chowla theorem also applies with $\lambda$ replaced by $\mu$.
Taking one relative shift fixed and averaging the other over
$1\le h\le h_j$, with the common translation $u_j=O(X_j)$ absorbed into
the allowed $b_r$, gives
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{h\le h_j}\left|
 \sum_{1\le n\le X_j}\mu(u_j+n)\mu(u_j+n+h)
 \right|=o(h_jX_j)=o(h_jL_j).
\end{equation}
For each $h\le h_j$, extending the inner sum from $X_j=L_j-h_j$ to
$L_j-h$ adds at most $h_j-h$ terms. Hence the total triangular boundary
cost is
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{h\le h_j}(h_j-h)=O(h_j^2)=o(h_jL_j),
\end{equation}
which proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Signed large-lag concentration in linear-scale excursions]
\label{cor:large-lag}
Let $B>0$ be fixed and let $I_j$ be excursions with $\ell_j\to\infty$,
$x_{0,j}\le B\ell_j$ and heights $H_j$ with $H_j^3/\ell_j\to\infty$. For every $h_j\to\infty$ with
$h_j^2\ell_j=o(H_j^3)$,
\begin{equation}
\sum_{h_j<h<\ell_j}h\,C^{(I_j)}_h=-(1+o(1))Q_{I_j}.
\end{equation}
If in addition $\Theta>1/2$, $0<\eta<\Theta-\frac12$ is fixed, there is a
fixed $\lambda'>1$ with $x_{1,j}\le\lambda' x_{0,j}$, and these excursions carry
Kaczorowski--Pintz witnesses
$H_j\ge\frac12x_{0,j}^{\Theta-\eta}$, then for every fixed
\begin{equation}
0<\theta_{\rm lag}<\frac{3(\Theta-\eta)-1}{2}
\end{equation}
one may take $h_j=x_{0,j}^{\theta_{\rm lag}}$, and hence
\begin{equation}
\sum_{x_{0,j}^{\theta_{\rm lag}}<h<\ell_j}h\,C_h^{(I_j)}=-(1+o(1))Q_{I_j}.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By Corollary~\CGref{cor:chowla-linear},
$\sum_{h\le h_j}|C^{(I_j)}_h|=\delta_jh_j\ell_j$ with $\delta_j\to0$.
Proposition~\CGref{prop:lag-localization} gives an error at most
$\delta_jh_j^2\ell_j=o(H_j^3)=o(Q_{I_j})$. In the witness case the hypotheses
$x_{0,j}\le B\ell_j$ and $x_{1,j}\le\lambda' x_{0,j}$ give
$\ell_j\asymp x_{0,j}$.  Hence
$H_j^3/\ell_j\gg x_{0,j}^{3\Theta-3\eta-1}$, so the displayed condition on
$\theta_{\rm lag}$ gives $h_j^2\ell_j=o(H_j^3)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Direction of the lag statement]
\label{rem:large-lag-direction}
Corollary~\CGref{cor:large-lag} says that the signed weighted correlation total representing the energy lies beyond every cutoff
$h_j$ satisfying $h_j=o(H_j^{3/2}\ell_j^{-1/2})$; it does \emph{not} say
that the energy is concentrated only at lags larger than the endpoint scale
$H_j^{3/2}\ell_j^{-1/2}$ itself.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Uniform two-point cancellation in sublinear intervals}
\label{subsec:sublinear-chowla}

The linear-scale restriction can be removed for mesoscopic lags by a
Fourier-uniform estimate valid in every interval in the range displayed
below. Theorem~1.5 of Matom\"aki--Ter\"av\"ainen
\cite{MatomakiTeravainen2023} gives, for every fixed $\iota>0$ and every
fixed $0<A<1/3$, a bound uniform in the additive frequency and in every
interval with $L\ge x^{3/5+\iota}$.  We use exactly that published range;
no stronger logarithmic exponent is assumed here.  Thus, for every fixed
$0<A<1/3$ and $\iota>0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:MT-fourier}
\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb R}\Bigl|\sum_{x<n\le x+L}\mu(n)e(\xi n)\Bigr|
\ll_{A,\iota}\frac{L}{(\log x)^A}
\qquad(x^{3/5+\iota}\le L\le x).
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform two-point Ces\`aro cancellation]
\label{thm:sublinear-two-point}
Fix $0<A<1/3$ and $\iota>0$. Uniformly for all integers $u,L,R$ with
$u^{3/5+\iota}\le L\le u$ and $1\le R\le L/2$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:sublinear-two-point}
\frac1R\sum_{h=1}^{R}\frac{|C^{(u,u+L]}_h|}{L-h}
\ll_{A,\iota}\Bigl(\frac LR\Bigr)^{1/2}(\log u)^{-A}.
\end{equation}
In particular, if $R\ge L(\log u)^{-2A+2\kappa}$ with
$0<\kappa<A$, the left side is $O((\log u)^{-\kappa})$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $a_i=\mu(u+i)$ for $1\le i\le L$ and
$A_I(\xi)=\sum_ia_ie(i\xi)$. For $h>0$ define
$C_{-h}^{(u,u+L]}:=\sum_{i=1}^{L-h}a_{i+h}a_i=C_h^{(u,u+L]}$ and put
$C_0=q=\sum_i a_i^2$.  These are the Fourier coefficients of
$|A_I(\xi)|^2$.  Parseval gives the precise identity
\begin{equation}
\int_0^1|A_I(\xi)|^4\,d\xi
=q^2+2\sum_{h=1}^{L-1}|C_h^{(u,u+L]}|^2
\le\|A_I\|_\infty^2q
\le L\|A_I\|_\infty^2.
\end{equation}
Hence Cauchy--Schwarz gives
$\sum_{h\le R}|C_h|\le\sqrt{RL}\,\|A_I\|_\infty$, and since
$L-h\ge L/2$ the left side of \CGeqref{eq:sublinear-two-point} is at most
$2\|A_I\|_\infty/\sqrt{RL}$. Apply \CGeqref{eq:MT-fourier}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Published mesoscopic logarithmic range]
\label{cor:near-log2-lags}
Fix $\iota>0$ and $0<\delta<2/3$. Uniformly for
\begin{equation}
u^{3/5+\iota}\le L\le u,
\qquad
\frac{L}{(\log u)^{2/3-\delta}}\le R\le\frac L2,
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{equation}
\frac1R\sum_{h\le R}\frac{|C^{(u,u+L]}_h|}{L-h}
\ll_{\iota,\delta}(\log u)^{-\delta/4}.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In Theorem~\CGref{thm:sublinear-two-point} take
$A=1/3-\delta/4$ and $\kappa=\delta/4$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Scope of the sublinear estimate]
\label{rem:sublinear-status}
Theorem~\CGref{thm:sublinear-two-point} is a short Parseval--Cauchy--Schwarz
corollary of the published Fourier estimate \CGeqref{eq:MT-fourier}; it does
not use the excursion condition. Its role here is to provide an arithmetic
input on every adaptively selected interval, something that ``almost all
intervals'' results cannot supply. The factor $(L/R)^{1/2}$ is inherent in
this direct $L^2$-to-$L^1$ Cauchy--Schwarz step using only the displayed
second-moment control of the $C_h$; additional information on the
distribution of the correlations could in principle improve it.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Sublinear large-lag estimate]
\label{cor:sublinear-large-lag}
Fix $\iota>0$ and $0<\delta<2/3$, and let $I=(u,u+L]$ be a completed
excursion with $u^{3/5+\iota}\le L\le u$.  For all sufficiently
large $u$, put
\begin{equation}
R=\left\lceil\frac{L}{(\log u)^{2/3-\delta}}\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
For sufficiently large $u$, $(\log u)^{2/3-\delta}\ge3$, and hence
$1\le R\le L/2$; therefore the preceding two-point estimate applies.
Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:sublinear-large-lag}
\sum_{R<h<L}hC_h^{(I)}
=-Q_I+O_{\iota,\delta}\!\left(
\frac{L^3}{(\log u)^{4/3-7\delta/4}}
\right).
\end{equation}
Consequently, if
$Q_I L^{-3}(\log u)^{4/3-7\delta/4}\to\infty$, then the left side is
$-(1+o(1))Q_I$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Corollary~\CGref{cor:near-log2-lags} gives
$\sum_{h\le R}|C_h^{(I)}|\ll RL(\log u)^{-\delta/4}$. Hence
\begin{equation}
\left|\sum_{h\le R}hC_h^{(I)}\right|
\le R\sum_{h\le R}|C_h^{(I)}|
\ll R^2L(\log u)^{-\delta/4},
\end{equation}
which is the error in \CGeqref{eq:sublinear-large-lag} after inserting the
chosen value of $R$ into $Q_I=-\sum_{h<L}hC_h^{(I)}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Connection with wide peaks]
\label{rem:sublinear-wide-peaks}
The quantitative form of the short-interval estimate in
\cite[Theorem~1.1]{MatomakiTeravainen2023} gives, after the blocking argument
of Lemma~\CGref{lem:meso-lipschitz},
$\omega_u\ll_\varepsilon(\log u)^{-1/3+\varepsilon}+u^{-c}$ for some $c>0$
(depending on the two fixed short-interval exponents used there). Thus, in
the regime $H/h_u\to\infty$ of Proposition~\CGref{prop:wide-peaks},
\begin{equation}
Q_I\gg_\varepsilon H^3(\log u)^{1/3-\varepsilon}.
\end{equation}
Combining this with Corollary~\CGref{cor:sublinear-large-lag}, a sufficient
condition for signed large-lag concentration is
\begin{equation}
\left(\frac HL\right)^3
(\log u)^{5/3-7\delta/4-\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\infty.
\end{equation}
For linearly localized Kaczorowski--Pintz excursions satisfying
$v\le\lambda u$ with a fixed $\lambda<2$, this machinery applies when
$\Theta>3/5$ (after choosing the loss parameters so that
$\Theta-\eta>3/5+\iota$); the remaining obstruction is that the excursion
may be much longer than its height.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Fej\'er reduction and the weaker local-variance target}

\begin{remark}[Excursion geometry alone does not give cancellation]
\label{rem:tent-chowla}
For the tent excursion $a_i=+1$ ($i\le k$), $a_i=-1$ ($k<i\le2k$), which
satisfies the bridge and one-sign constraints of a primitive excursion,
$C_h=2k-3h$ for $1\le h<k$. Hence for $R\to\infty$, $R=o(k)$,
\begin{equation}
\frac1R\sum_{h\le R}\frac{|C_h|}{2k-h}\longrightarrow1,
\qquad
\sum_{h<R}(R-h)C_h\asymp kR^2,
\end{equation}
whereas $RL\asymp kR$. Arithmetic input is indispensable.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Finite Fej\'er identity, classical]
\label{lem:fejer}
For a real sequence $(a_i)_{1\le i\le L}$, extended by $0$ outside
$[1,L]$, put
\begin{equation}
q=\sum_{i=1}^La_i^2,
\qquad
C_h=\sum_{i=1}^{L-h}a_ia_{i+h},
\qquad
F_R:=\sum_{h=1}^{R-1}(R-h)C_h.
\end{equation}
Then for $1\le R\le L$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:fejer}
Rq+2F_R
=\sum_{m\in\mathbb Z}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{R}a_{m+j}\right)^2.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Expand the square. Each diagonal term occurs $R$ times, and a pair at lag
$h$, $1\le h<R$, occurs $2(R-h)$ times.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Bridge--Fej\'er identity and interior variance]
\label{prop:bridge-fejer}
Let $I=(u,u+L]$ be a completed excursion, put
$a_i=\mu(u+i)$ and
$S_t=\sum_{i=1}^ta_i=M(u+t)$ for $0\le t\le L$. Then
$S_0=S_L=0$, and for every $1\le R<L$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:bridge-fejer-Q}
Rq+2F_R
=2Q_I-2\sum_{t=1}^{L-1-R}S_tS_{t+R}.
\end{equation}
Moreover
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:fejer-interior-boundary}
Rq+2F_R
=E_{\rm bd}(R)
+\sum_{m=0}^{L-R}\bigl(M(u+m+R)-M(u+m)\bigr)^2,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
E_{\rm bd}(R)=\sum_{t=1}^{R-1}S_t^2
+\sum_{t=L-R+1}^{L-1}S_t^2
=O(R^3).
\end{equation}
Consequently, if $R=o(\sqrt L)$, then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:fejer-variance-equivalence}
F_R=o(RL)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\sum_{m=0}^{L-R}\bigl(M(u+m+R)-M(u+m)\bigr)^2
=Rq+o(RL).
\end{equation}
If in addition $L\ge u^{1/2+\eps}$ and $L\le u$, then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:q-squarefree-local}
q=\left(\frac6{\pi^2}+o(1)\right)L,
\end{equation}
and the right side of \CGeqref{eq:fejer-variance-equivalence} is equivalently
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:local-variance-diagonal}
\sum_{m=0}^{L-R}\bigl(M(u+m+R)-M(u+m)\bigr)^2
=\left(\frac6{\pi^2}+o(1)\right)RL.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Extend $S_t$ by $0$ outside $0\le t\le L$. Since
$\sum_{j=1}^Ra_{m+j}=S_{m+R}-S_m$, \CGeqref{eq:fejer} gives
\begin{equation}
Rq+2F_R=\sum_m(S_{m+R}-S_m)^2
=2\sum_mS_m^2-2\sum_mS_mS_{m+R},
\end{equation}
The natural range of the last sum is $0\le m\le L-R$, but the terms
$m=0$ and $m=L-R$ vanish because $S_0=S_L=0$, leaving $1\le t\le L-1-R$;
this is \CGeqref{eq:bridge-fejer-Q}. Splitting the same sum into $m<0$, $0\le m\le L-R$, and $m>L-R$
gives \CGeqref{eq:fejer-interior-boundary}: for $-R<m<0$ the contribution is
$S_{m+R}^2$, for $L-R<m<L$ it is $S_m^2$, and the endpoint terms vanish
because $S_0=S_L=0$.  Since $|a_i|\le1$ and the path returns
to zero, $|S_t|\le t$ and $|S_{L-t}|\le t$, whence
$E_{\rm bd}(R)\le2\sum_{t<R}t^2=O(R^3)$. Thus
$E_{\rm bd}(R)=o(RL)$ when $R=o(\sqrt L)$, proving the equivalence.
Finally the classical estimate
$\sum_{n\le x}\mu(n)^2=(6/\pi^2)x+O(\sqrt x)$ gives
$q=(6/\pi^2)L+O(\sqrt{u+L})$.  Under
$u^{1/2+\eps}\le L\le u$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\sqrt{u+L}}{L}\le
 \frac{\sqrt{2u}}{u^{1/2+\eps}}\ll u^{-\eps},
\end{equation}
so the error is uniform and \CGeqref{eq:q-squarefree-local} follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Fej\'er density at frequency zero]
\label{rem:fejer-spectrum}
With $A_I(\xi)=\sum_{i=1}^La_ie(i\xi)$ and
\begin{equation}
K_R(\xi)=\frac1R\left|\sum_{j=1}^Re(j\xi)\right|^2,
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{equation}
q+\frac{2F_R}{R}=\int_0^1|A_I(\xi)|^2K_R(\xi)\,d\xi.
\end{equation}
Thus $F_R=o(RL)$ says that the Fej\'er average of the local spectral density
at frequency $0$, at resolution $1/R$, equals $q+o(L)$. It is not a
statement that $|A_I(\xi)|^2$ is spectrally flat on the whole circle.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Three distinct targets]
\label{rem:three-chowla-targets}
The following conditions must not be conflated.

First, absolute averaged Chowla,
$\sum_{h<R}|C_h|=o(RL)$, implies only
$|F_R|\le R\sum_{h<R}|C_h|=o(R^2L)$, not the sharper
$F_R=o(RL)$ when $R\to\infty$. Conversely, $F_R=o(RL)$ allows cancellation
between the $C_h$ and does not control their absolute values.

Second, $F_R$ does not by itself control the small-lag contribution to the
excursion energy. If
\begin{equation}
V_R:=\sum_{h<R}hC_h,
\end{equation}
then exactly
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:F-V-relation}
F_R+V_R=R\sum_{h<R}C_h.
\end{equation}
Thus a Fej\'er estimate alone does not imply $V_R=o(Q_I)$.

Third, Proposition~\CGref{prop:bridge-fejer} requires both endpoints of the
block to be zeros of $M$. For a block beginning at a zero but ending before
the return, additional boundary terms remain.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Why the variance target may go below $3/5$]
\label{rem:variance-target}
For the Fej\'er problem one does not need the stronger absolute estimate
$\sum|C_h|$. By Proposition~\CGref{prop:bridge-fejer}, on a completed excursion
and for $R=o(\sqrt L)$ it is enough to prove the diagonal local-variance law
\begin{equation}
\sum_{m=0}^{L-R}\bigl(M(u+m+R)-M(u+m)\bigr)^2
=Rq+o(RL).
\end{equation}
This is a different signed target from absolute averaged Chowla: neither
implication is supplied by the estimates above.  It is a plausible target
below the Fourier-uniform threshold $L\ge u^{3/5+\iota}$.
The all-interval $U^2$/Fourier estimates currently used above do not, by
themselves, supply this asymptotic for every adaptively selected block in
the range $L\ge u^{1/2+\eps}$. Results holding for almost all starting
points cannot be substituted, because excursion endpoints are selected by
$M$ itself.

No fixed-prefix formula for the increments in
\CGeqref{eq:local-variance-diagonal} is asserted here. In particular, the
identity $\sum_{d\le x}\mu(d)\lfloor x/d\rfloor=1$ introduces lower-scale
terms such as $M(x/2)$ when one truncates at $x/2$; omitting those terms
would give an invalid reduction.
\end{remark}



\subsection{Dyadic multiscale Fej\'er forcing on completed excursions}
\label{subsec:multiscale-fejer}

The preceding Fej\'er identity treats one scale at a time. A completed
one-sign excursion permits a second operation: close the zero-padded block
into a cycle. The wrap-around terms then have a favorable sign, and the
whole dyadic tower can be combined without paying an unsigned boundary
error.

Let $I=(u,u+L]$ be a completed excursion with orientation $\sigma$. Put
\begin{equation}
 a_i=\sigma\mu(u+i)\quad(1\le i\le L),
 \qquad a_i=0\quad(L<i\le\widehat L),
\end{equation}
where $\widehat L=2^{m_{\rm dy}}$ is the least power of two not smaller than
$L$. Write
\begin{equation}
 S_r=\sum_{i=1}^r a_i\quad(0\le r\le\widehat L),
 \qquad q=\sum_{i=1}^{\widehat L}a_i^2.
\end{equation}
Then $S_r\ge0$, $S_L=0$, and in fact $S_r=0$ for
$L\le r\le\widehat L$. For $1\le h<\widehat L$ let
\begin{equation}
 C_h=\sum_{i=1}^{\widehat L-h}a_i a_{i+h},
 \qquad
 F_R=\sum_{h=1}^{R-1}(R-h)C_h.
\end{equation}
All variables introduced here are local except the already defined $F_R$ and
the multiscale sum $\mathfrak K_I$ below.

\begin{lemma}[Cyclic Fej\'er boundary sign]
\label{lem:cyclic-fejer-boundary}
Extend $(a_i)$ periodically modulo $\widehat L$ and define
\begin{equation}
 C_{h}^{\rm cyc}=\sum_{i=1}^{\widehat L}a_i a_{i+h},
 \qquad
 F_R^{\rm cyc}=\sum_{h=1}^{R-1}(R-h)C_h^{\rm cyc},
\end{equation}
where the index $i+h$ is read modulo $\widehat L$. For every
$1\le R\le\widehat L/2$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cyclic-fejer-boundary}
 \boxed{
 F_R^{\rm cyc}
 =F_R-\sum_{r=1}^{R-1}S_rS_{\widehat L-R+r}
 \le F_R.}
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}
 W_R^{\rm cyc}:=
 \sum_{s=0}^{\widehat L-1}
 \left(\sum_{j=1}^{R}a_{s+j}\right)^2,
\end{equation}
with cyclic indices, then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cyclic-window-square}
 \boxed{W_R^{\rm cyc}=Rq+2F_R^{\rm cyc}.}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $1\le h<R$ the only terms added by cyclic closure are the pairs that
cross the cut between $\widehat L$ and $1$, hence
\begin{equation}
 C_h^{\rm cyc}-C_h
 =\sum_{i=\widehat L-h+1}^{\widehat L}
   a_i a_{i+h-\widehat L}.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{align}
 F_R^{\rm cyc}-F_R
 &=\sum_{h=1}^{R-1}(R-h)
   \sum_{i=\widehat L-h+1}^{\widehat L}
   a_i a_{i+h-\widehat L}\\
 &=\sum_{j=1}^{R-1}\sum_{i=\widehat L-R+j+1}^{\widehat L}
   \bigl(R-(\widehat L-i+j)\bigr)a_i a_j,
\end{align}
where in the second line $j=i+h-\widehat L$ and the inequalities
$1\le h<R$ have been rewritten in $(i,j)$ coordinates.  On the other hand,
$S_{\widehat L}=0$ gives
\begin{equation*}
 S_{\widehat L-R+r}
 =-\sum_{i=\widehat L-R+r+1}^{\widehat L}a_i,
\tag{S12.1}
\end{equation*}
and hence
\begin{align}
 -\sum_{r=1}^{R-1}S_rS_{\widehat L-R+r}
 &=\sum_{r=1}^{R-1}\sum_{j=1}^{r}
   \sum_{i=\widehat L-R+r+1}^{\widehat L}a_j a_i\\
 &=\sum_{j=1}^{R-1}\sum_{i=\widehat L-R+j+1}^{\widehat L}
   \bigl(R-(\widehat L-i+j)\bigr)a_i a_j.
\end{align}
The two displayed double sums are identical, proving
\CGeqref{eq:cyclic-fejer-boundary}.  Because the zero-padded oriented
excursion has $S_r\ge0$ and also
$S_{\widehat L-R+r}\ge0$, the boundary term is nonnegative and
$F_R^{\rm cyc}\le F_R$.

Finally expand $W_R^{\rm cyc}$.  Each $a_i^2$ occurs in exactly $R$ cyclic
windows.  For a cyclic pair at lag $h<R$, the product $a_i a_{i+h}$ occurs
in exactly $R-h$ windows in each orientation, hence with total coefficient
$2(R-h)$.  This gives
$W_R^{\rm cyc}=Rq+2\sum_{h<R}(R-h)C_h^{\rm cyc}$ and proves
\CGeqref{eq:cyclic-window-square}.
\end{proof}

Define the dyadic scale set
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R_I:=\{1,2,4,\ldots,\widehat L/2\}
\end{equation}
and the signed multiscale Fej\'er sum
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak K_I:=\sum_{R\in\mathcal R_I}\frac{F_R}{R}.
\end{equation}
There are exactly $m_{\rm dy}$ scales in $\mathcal R_I$.

\begin{proposition}[Dyadic multiscale Fej\'er inequality]
\label{prop:multiscale-fejer}
With the preceding notation and $H=\max_{0\le r\le L}S_r$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:multiscale-fejer}
 \boxed{
 H^2
 \le m_{\rm dy}\sum_{R\in\mathcal R_I}\frac{W_R^{\rm cyc}}{R}
 \le m_{\rm dy}\bigl(m_{\rm dy}q+2\mathfrak K_I\bigr).}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:multiscale-K-lower}
 \boxed{
 \mathfrak K_I
 \ge \frac{H^2}{2m_{\rm dy}}-\frac{m_{\rm dy}q}{2}.}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
View $S$ periodically on $\mathbb Z/\widehat L\mathbb Z$ and put
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal A_RS(s):=\frac1R\sum_{t=0}^{R-1}S_{s+t}
 \qquad(R\in\mathcal R_I).
\end{equation}
For $2R\le\widehat L$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal A_RS(s)-\mathcal A_{2R}S(s)
 =-\frac1{2R}\sum_{t=0}^{R-1}\bigl(S_{s+t+R}-S_{s+t}\bigr).
\end{equation}
By Cauchy--Schwarz and the definition of $W_R^{\rm cyc}$,
\begin{equation}
 \left|\mathcal A_RS(s)-\mathcal A_{2R}S(s)\right|^2
 \le \frac{W_R^{\rm cyc}}{4R}.
\end{equation}
Telescoping from $R=1$ to $R=\widehat L$ and writing
$\overline S=\widehat L^{-1}\sum_{s=0}^{\widehat L-1}S_s$ gives
\begin{equation}
 |S_s-\overline S|
 \le\frac12\sum_{R\in\mathcal R_I}
 \left(\frac{W_R^{\rm cyc}}R\right)^{1/2}
 \le\frac12\left(
 m_{\rm dy}\sum_{R\in\mathcal R_I}\frac{W_R^{\rm cyc}}R
 \right)^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
Apply this once at $s=0$, where $S_0=0$, and once at an apex $s_*$ with
$S_{s_*}=H$. The triangle inequality then yields
\begin{equation}
 H\le |H-\overline S|+|\overline S|
 \le\left(
 m_{\rm dy}\sum_{R\in\mathcal R_I}\frac{W_R^{\rm cyc}}R
 \right)^{1/2},
\end{equation}
which is the first inequality in \CGeqref{eq:multiscale-fejer}. By
Lemma~\CGref{lem:cyclic-fejer-boundary},
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{R\in\mathcal R_I}\frac{W_R^{\rm cyc}}R
 =m_{\rm dy}q+2\sum_{R\in\mathcal R_I}\frac{F_R^{\rm cyc}}R
 \le m_{\rm dy}q+2\mathfrak K_I.
\end{equation}
This proves the second inequality and, after rearrangement,
\CGeqref{eq:multiscale-K-lower}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[RH violation forces multiscale correlation excess]
\label{cor:RH-violation-multiscale}
Assume $\Theta>1/2$. Fix
\begin{equation}
 \frac12<\alpha<\Theta
 \qquad\text{and}\qquad
 \lambda>\lambda_0.
\end{equation}
Then there is an unbounded family of completed excursions
$I=(u,v]$ with $v\le\lambda u$ for which
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RH-violation-multiscale}
 \boxed{
 \mathfrak K_I
 \gg_{\alpha,\lambda}\frac{u^{2\alpha}}{\log u}.}
\end{equation}
In particular, failure of RH forces a positive correlation excess across the
dyadic Fej\'er scales; the diagonal term $m_{\rm dy}q=O_\lambda(u\log u)$
cannot sustain the required heights.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Choose $\eta>0$ so that $\alpha<\Theta-\eta$. By
Corollary~\CGref{cor:KP-large-excursions}, the number of large-witness
excursions starting below $X$ is at least
$(T_0/\pi-o(1))\log X$. By Lemma~\CGref{lem:linear-lacunary}, the number
among them with $v>\lambda u$ is at most
$(1/\log\lambda+o(1))\log X$. Since
$\lambda>e^{\pi/T_0}$, the difference of these two coefficients is positive;
hence an unbounded family remains with $v\le\lambda u$. On each such
excursion a witness $t\in(u,v]$ satisfies
$|M(t)|\ge\frac12t^{\Theta-\eta}$, and therefore, for all sufficiently large
$u$, its height satisfies $H\ge u^\alpha$. Also
$L=v-u\le(\lambda-1)u$, so $q\le L=O_\lambda(u)$ and, because
$L\le\widehat L<2L$, one has $m_{\rm dy}=O_\lambda(\log u)$. Insert these
bounds into \CGeqref{eq:multiscale-K-lower}; since $2\alpha>1$, the
$u^{2\alpha}/\log u$ term eventually dominates $u\log u$, proving
\CGeqref{eq:RH-violation-multiscale}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Open multiscale Fej\'er target]
\label{rem:multiscale-fejer-target}
The preceding corollary is a reduction, not a proof of RH. A concrete
arithmetic estimate that would close this route is the following: for some
fixed $\lambda>\lambda_0$ and some finite $A$, prove uniformly for every
sufficiently late completed excursion $I=(u,v]$ with $v\le\lambda u$ that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:multiscale-fejer-target}
 \boxed{
 \mathfrak K_I\ll_{\lambda,A}u(\log u)^A.}
\end{equation}
Together with Corollary~\CGref{cor:RH-violation-multiscale}, this would rule
out every $\Theta>1/2$. The Fej\'er weights themselves are classical, but
\CGeqref{eq:multiscale-fejer-target} is not a theorem used in this paper and
is not claimed here as a known or bibliographically new result. It is the
open signed multiscale estimate isolated by the completed-excursion
reduction. Existing small-lag or one-scale controls do not automatically
imply it.
\end{remark}


\section{Extreme values in stochastic models of excursions}
\label{sec:extreme}
\label{subsec:extreme}

The counts of Section~\CGref{subsec:geometric} bound the number of excursions
in a family, not their heights. They acquire a second function when combined
with a tail hypothesis on a normalized excursion statistic: they fix the
scale of the family maximum, with no independence assumption.

Let $(\mathfrak X,\mathcal G,\Pr)$ be a probability space carrying a random
sequence of disjoint integer intervals $I_k=(x_{0,k},x_{1,k}]$ with
$x_{1,k}\le x_{0,k+1}$, lengths at least $2$, starts in
$\{0,2,3,\ldots\}$, and real random variables $Z_k$ (for instance a model
of the M\"obius walk with $Z_k=H_k/\sqrt{\ell_k}$). A family $\F$ is given by
a rule selecting indices from $(x_{0,k},x_{1,k})_k$, such as $\F_{\mathfrak h}$; let
$k_\F(1)<k_\F(2)<\cdots$ enumerate its members, and put
\begin{equation}
Z_{\max,\F}(X):=\max\{Z_k:k\in\F,\ x_{0,k}\le X\}\qquad(\max\emptyset=-\infty).
\end{equation}
The lemmas of Section~\CGref{subsec:geometric} hold for every realization.

\begin{hypothesis}[$\mathrm{SG}_\varpi$]
\label{hyp:SG}
There are $C,\kappa_0,\varpi>0$ such that for every $j\ge1$ and $z\ge0$
\begin{equation}
\Pr\bigl(k_\F(j)\text{ exists and }Z_{k_\F(j)}>z\bigr)\le Ce^{-\kappa_0z^\varpi}.
\end{equation}
For $\varpi=2$ this is a uniform sub-Gaussian tail, denoted (SG).
\end{hypothesis}

\begin{theorem}[Extreme scale from a count]
\label{thm:ev-union}
Assume $(\mathrm{SG}_\varpi)$ for $\F$ and suppose that $E_\F(X)\le \mathcal N(X)$ for
every realization, with $\mathcal N(X)\ge1$ deterministic. Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ev-union}
\Pr\bigl(Z_{\max,\F}(X)>z\bigr)\le \mathcal N(X)\,Ce^{-\kappa_0z^\varpi}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every $\delta>0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ev-scale}
\Pr\Bigl(Z_{\max,\F}(X)>\Bigl(\frac{1+\delta}{\kappa_0}\log \mathcal N(X)\Bigr)^{1/\varpi}\Bigr)
\le C\,\mathcal N(X)^{-\delta},
\end{equation}
and $\max\{0,Z_{\max,\F}(X)\}=O_{\Pr}\bigl((\log \mathcal N(X))^{1/\varpi}\bigr)$ as $\mathcal N(X)\to\infty$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The members of $\F$ starting at most $X$ are the first $E_\F(X)\le \mathcal N(X)$
members of the enumeration, so
$\{Z_{\max,\F}(X)>z\}\subset\bigcup_{1\le j\le\lfloor\mathcal N(X)\rfloor}\{k_\F(j)\text{ exists},\
Z_{k_\F(j)}>z\}$, and \CGeqref{eq:ev-union} is the union bound. Substituting
$\kappa_0z^\varpi=(1+\delta)\log \mathcal N(X)$ gives \CGeqref{eq:ev-scale}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Triple-logarithmic scale on the square family]
\label{cor:ev-square}
Assume (SG) for the square-exceptional family $\F_{x^2}$ of the model, and
write $Z_{\max,\square}:=Z_{\max,\F_{x^2}}$. Then, for $X\ge e^e$ and every
$\delta>0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ev-square}
\Pr\bigl(Z_{\max,\square}(X)>z\bigr)\le4C(\log\log X)e^{-\kappa_0z^2}.
\end{equation}
For all sufficiently large $X$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ev-square-b}
\Pr\Bigl(Z_{\max,\square}(X)>\sqrt{\tfrac{1+\delta}{\kappa_0}\log\log\log X}\Bigr)
\le4C(\log\log X)^{-\delta}.
\end{equation}
Thus, for all sufficiently large $X$,
$\max\{0,Z_{\max,\square}(X)\}=O_{\Pr}(\sqrt{\log\log\log X})$. The same holds for
the family $\F_{cx^2}$ of Lemma~\CGref{lem:partition} with a constant depending
on $c$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Use \CGeqref{eq:square-exception-count} in \CGeqref{eq:ev-union} and note that
$e^{-\kappa_0z^2}=(\log\log X)^{-(1+\delta)}$ at the stated $z$.
\end{proof}

The union bound gives convergence in probability; a Borel--Cantelli argument
along a sequence adapted to $\mathcal N$ gives an almost sure bound with the same
constant.

\begin{theorem}[Almost sure extreme scale]
\label{thm:ev-as}
Assume $(\mathrm{SG}_\varpi)$ for $\F$ and $E_\F(X)\le \mathcal N(X)$ for every realization,
where $\mathcal N$ is continuous, nondecreasing and unbounded. Then almost surely
\begin{equation}
\limsup_{X\to\infty}\frac{Z_{\max,\F}(X)}{(\log \mathcal N(X))^{1/\varpi}}\le \kappa_0^{-1/\varpi}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\delta>0$. For $j\ge j_0$ let $X_j:=\inf\{X:\mathcal N(X)\ge2^j\}$.
By continuity, monotonicity and unboundedness, after increasing $j_0$ if
necessary one has $\mathcal N(X_j)=2^j$. Put $z_j=\bigl((1+\delta)(j+1)\log2/\kappa_0\bigr)^{1/\varpi}$ and
$A_j=\{Z_{\max,\F}(X_{j+1})>z_j\}$. By \CGeqref{eq:ev-union},
$\Pr(A_j)\le2^{j+1}Ce^{-(1+\delta)(j+1)\log2}=C2^{-(j+1)\delta}$, which is
summable, so almost surely $A_j$ occurs only finitely often. For
$X_j\le X\le X_{j+1}$, monotonicity of $Z_{\max,\F}$ in $X$ gives
$Z_{\max,\F}(X)\le z_j$ outside $A_j$, while $\log \mathcal N(X)\ge j\log2$. Hence
the $\limsup$ is at most
$\lim_j\bigl((1+\delta)(j+1)/(\kappa_0j)\bigr)^{1/\varpi}=((1+\delta)/\kappa_0)^{1/\varpi}$. Let
$\delta\downarrow0$ along a countable sequence.
\end{proof}

With $\mathcal N_\square(X)=2+\log_2(\log X/\log2)$, a continuous
increasing majorant of the integer counting function,
Theorem~\CGref{thm:ev-as} gives under (SG)
\begin{equation}
\limsup_{X\to\infty}\frac{Z_{\max,\square}(X)}{\sqrt{\log\log\log X}}\le \kappa_0^{-1/2}
\quad\text{almost surely.}
\end{equation}
Applied to the three counts available for any sequence of disjoint
intervals (all excursions: at most $X+1$; Lemma~\CGref{lem:linear-lacunary};
Lemma~\CGref{lem:power-lacunary}), Theorems~\CGref{thm:ev-union}
and~\CGref{thm:ev-as} give three levels:
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
\text{family} & \text{members starting}\le X & \text{extreme scale under (SG)}\\
\hline
\text{all excursions} & \le X+1 & \sqrt{\log X}\\
x_1>\lambda x_0 & O(\log X) & \sqrt{\log\log X}\\
x_1>cx_0^2\ \ (\text{or }x_0^A) & O(\log\log X) & \sqrt{\log\log\log X}
\end{array}
\end{equation}
Each level of lacunarity removes one logarithm from the extreme scale.
Under $(\mathrm{SG}_\varpi)$ the last column reads $(\log X)^{1/\varpi}$,
$(\log\log X)^{1/\varpi}$, $(\log\log\log X)^{1/\varpi}$.

\begin{remark}[Scope]
\label{rem:ev-scope}
For $M$ itself the square-exceptional family is finite by
Corollary~\CGref{cor:KP-local-returns}, so
Theorems~\CGref{thm:ev-union}--\CGref{thm:ev-as} concern stochastic models of
the M\"obius walk, in which the pathwise counts of
Section~\CGref{subsec:geometric} persist while no analogue of the
Kaczorowski--Pintz localization is available. Hypothesis (SG) is an
assumption on the model; the theorems state exactly what the counts
contribute to its extremes.
\end{remark}


\section{A one-frequency model for the net area curve}
\label{sec:model}

\begin{heuristic}[Scale and period]
\label{heu:model}
Motivated by \CGeqref{eq:explicitM}, model $M(x)\approx\sqrt x\,F(\log x)$
with $F$ almost periodic, and retain only the lowest zero $\gamma_1$ (RH
does not imply that its coefficient dominates). Within an excursion of
length $\asymp x$ this gives $H_\nu\asymp\sqrt x$ and
$\sqrt{H_\nu}\ell_\nu\asymp x^{5/4}$, so $\Anet$ oscillates at scale
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:model_scale}
  |\Anet(x)|\asymp x^{5/4},
\end{equation}
the scale of Corollary~\CGref{cor:rh}, with period $2\pi/\gamma_1$ in
$\log x$, i.e.\ a factor $e^{2\pi/\gamma_1}\approx1.5597$ in $x$. In this
model consecutive zeros of $M$ are separated by the factor
$e^{\pi/\gamma_1}=\lambda_*$.  This is the uniform Corollary~4 benchmark of the counting comparison, not the current verified-height horizon $\lambda_0$; in the one-frequency model a model excursion has endpoint ratio exactly $\lambda_*$, at the boundary
of the strict $\lambda_*$-long condition.
\end{heuristic}



\section{Column, row, and diagonal phase sums indexed by zeta zeros}
\label{sec:diagonal-zero-phases}

This section is diagnostic and is logically separate from the excursion
identities above. Write $\rho_j=\beta_j+i\gamma_j$ for the nontrivial zeros
with $\gamma_j>0$, ordered by ordinate and counted with multiplicity, and put
\begin{equation*}
 \mathscr P_{m,j}:=m^{i\gamma_j},\qquad m,j\ge1.
\tag{S15.1}
\end{equation*}
A column fixes a zero and varies the integer; a row fixes an integer and sums
over zeros; the diagonal pairs the integer $k$ with the $k$th zero. These are
three mathematically different operations.

For a fixed zero on the critical line, $\beta_j=1/2$, Euler--Maclaurin gives (with $\gamma_j$ fixed; this is not a uniform estimate as $\gamma_j\to\infty$)
(the same expansion used in \CGeqref{eq:dirichlet-partial-sum})
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:zero-phase-column}
 \sum_{m\le K}m^{-1/2}\mathscr P_{m,j}
 -\frac{K^{1/2+i\gamma_j}}{1/2+i\gamma_j}
 =\zeta\!\left(\tfrac12-i\gamma_j\right)+O_{\gamma_j}(K^{-1/2})
 \longrightarrow 0.
\end{equation}
Thus the zero is detected by the renormalized constant term, not by the
unsubtracted partial sum, whose boundary term grows like $K^{1/2}$. For an
off-line zero the corresponding zeta weight is $m^{-\beta_j}$, not the fixed
weight $m^{-1/2}$.

For a fixed integer $m\ge2$, summing a row over zeros is governed by Landau's
formula. In the fixed-$m$ form recorded by Kaczorowski--Languasco--Perelli
\cite{KLP2000},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:landau-zero-row}
 \sum_{0<\gamma_j\le T}m^{\rho_j}
 =-\frac{T}{2\pi}\Lambda(m)+O_m(\log T)
 \qquad(T\to\infty).
\end{equation}
Here $\Lambda(m)$ is nonzero precisely for prime powers. Under RH,
$m^{\rho_j}=m^{1/2}\mathscr P_{m,j}$, and hence
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:landau-phase-row}
 \sum_{0<\gamma_j\le T}\mathscr P_{m,j}
 =-\frac{T}{2\pi}\frac{\Lambda(m)}{\sqrt m}+O_m(\log T).
\end{equation}
The pure-phase form \CGeqref{eq:landau-phase-row} therefore carries an RH
hypothesis; the unconditional row formula is \CGeqref{eq:landau-zero-row}.

\subsection{The diagonal is ordinal, not arbitrary}
\label{subsec:diagonal-ordinal}

Neither the column identity nor Landau's row formula evaluates the diagonal,
but the pairing $k\leftrightarrow\gamma_k$ is not arbitrary: $k$ is the
counting index of the zero. If $N_\zeta(T)$ denotes the positive-ordinate
zero-counting function, then
$N_\zeta(\gamma_k^- )<k\le N_\zeta(\gamma_k)$ for the right-continuous
count.  Equality $k=N_\zeta(\gamma_k)$ holds for the last occurrence at
that ordinate; the difference for other occurrences is bounded by the total
number of zero occurrences at that height. The Riemann--von Mangoldt formula \cite{Titchmarsh1986} gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-smooth-count}
 N_\zeta(T)=\mathcal N_{\rm RVM,sm}(T)+O(\log T),\qquad
 \mathcal N_{\rm RVM,sm}(T):=
 \frac{T}{2\pi}\log\frac{T}{2\pi e}+\frac78.
\end{equation}
Consequently $\gamma_k\sim2\pi k/\log k$. The inverse of the smooth main
term is explicit through the principal Lambert $W$-function
(see \href{https://dlmf.nist.gov/4.13}{DLMF~\S4.13}): for real $x>7/8$ set
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-smooth-clock}
 \bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}(x):=
 \frac{2\pi(x-7/8)}{W_0((x-7/8)/e)},\qquad
 \bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k:=\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}(k),
\end{equation}
so that $\mathcal N_{\rm RVM,sm}(\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k)=k$. Thus the diagonal mixes a
strong deterministic ordinal component with the fluctuations of the actual
zero ordinates around this smooth clock.

With a weight exponent $\alpha_{\rm ph}\ge0$, chosen independently of the
real parts $\beta_k$, define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:diagonal-zero-sum}
 \mathscr D_{\alpha_{\rm ph}}(N)
 :=\sum_{k=1}^{N}k^{-\alpha_{\rm ph}}\mathscr P_{k,k}
 =\sum_{k=1}^{N}k^{-\alpha_{\rm ph}}e^{i\gamma_k\log k}.
\end{equation}
No analogue of the column or row explicit formula is asserted here for
\CGeqref{eq:diagonal-zero-sum}. In particular, $\alpha_{\rm ph}=1/2$ is an
experimental normalization; it is not, by itself, a test of RH or of
$\Theta=1/2$.

The smooth clock permits an exact separation of the two ingredients. Define,
locally in this section,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:diagonal-phase-fluctuation}
 \chi_k^{\rm RVM,ph}:=
 e^{i(\gamma_k-\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k)\log k}.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:diagonal-smooth-fluctuation-factorization}
 e^{i\gamma_k\log k}
 =e^{i\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k\log k}\chi_k^{\rm RVM,ph}.
\end{equation}
The factor $e^{i\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k\log k}$ is forced by the smooth inverse
zero-counting law; $\chi_k^{\rm RVM,ph}$ carries the displacement of the actual
ordinate from that clock.

Two different averages of the diagonal path must also be distinguished:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:diagonal-centroids}
 C_{\alpha_{\rm ph}}^{\rm pts}(N)
 :=\frac{\mathscr D_{\alpha_{\rm ph}}(N)}{N},\qquad
 C_{\alpha_{\rm ph}}^{\rm path}(N)
 :=\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathscr D_{\alpha_{\rm ph}}(n)
 =\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{N-k+1}{N}
   k^{-\alpha_{\rm ph}}e^{i\gamma_k\log k}.
\end{equation}
The first averages the points (equivalently the terminal sum divided by
$N$); the second averages all partial sums of the path. At
$\alpha_{\rm ph}=1/2$, the supplied computation at $N=2\,001\,052$ gives
\begin{equation*}
 C_{1/2}^{\rm pts}(N)\approx-8.626\cdot10^{-7}
 +1.291\cdot10^{-6}i,\qquad
 C_{1/2}^{\rm path}(N)\approx-2.461+2.493i.
\tag{S15.2}
\end{equation*}
The small point average is forced by normalization:
\begin{equation*}
 |C_{1/2}^{\rm pts}(N)|\le \frac1N\sum_{k\le N}k^{-1/2}
 =O(N^{-1/2}),
\tag{S15.3}
\end{equation*}
independently of the zeta ordinates. The displayed value of
$C_{1/2}^{\rm path}(N)$ is a finite-$N$ observation, not a proved limiting
value; its regeneration file and checksum are recorded in the v52 computational audit package.

\subsection{The smooth clock and the obstruction for the actual ordinates}
\label{subsec:diagonal-smooth-clock}

The smooth clock itself has enough phase drift to make the weighted diagonal
series converge. This fact is useful mainly because it isolates what remains
to be controlled for the actual ordinates.


\begin{lemma}[Derivative of the smooth diagonal phase]
\label{lem:smooth-clock-phase-derivative}
Let $\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}(x)$ be the inverse of the smooth Riemann--von Mangoldt main
term and put
\begin{equation*}
 \mathscr H_{\rm RVM,ph}(x):=\frac{\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}(x)\log x}{2\pi}.
\tag{S15.4}
\end{equation*}
Then, as $x\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:smooth-clock-phase-derivative-lemma}
 \mathscr H_{\rm RVM,ph}'(x)
 =1+\frac{\log\log x+1}{\log x}
 +O\!\left(\frac{(\log\log x)^2}{(\log x)^2}\right),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
 \frac{d}{dx}\bigl(\mathscr H_{\rm RVM,ph}'(x)-1\bigr)
 =-\frac{\log\log x+O(1)}{x(\log x)^2}<0
\end{equation}
for all sufficiently large $x$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Writing $y=(x-7/8)/e$ and using the principal Lambert function in the exact
inverse formula for $\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}(x)$, the standard expansion
$W_0(y)=\log y-\log\log y+O(\log\log y/\log y)$ gives
\begin{equation*}
 \bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}(x)=\frac{2\pi x}{\log x}
 \left(1+\frac{\log\log x+1+O(1/\log\log x)}{\log x}
\right).
\tag{S15.5}
\end{equation*}
To justify differentiation, use the exact identities, with
$y=x-7/8$ and $w=W_0(y/e)$,
\begin{equation*}
 w'=\frac{w}{y(1+w)},\qquad
 \mathscr H_{\rm RVM,ph}'(x)=\frac{\log x}{1+w}+\frac{y}{xw}.
\tag{S15.6}
\end{equation*}
Differentiating this exact formula and then expanding $w$ gives both
displays, with derivative error
$O((\log\log x)^2/(x(\log x)^3))$.  No differentiation of a bare
$O$-term is being assumed.  In particular
$\mathscr H_{\rm RVM,ph}'(x)>1$ and decreases to $1$ from above eventually.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Convergence for the smooth Riemann--von Mangoldt clock]
\label{prop:smooth-clock-convergence}
After an arbitrary choice of finitely many initial terms, the series
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:smooth-clock-series}
 \mathscr D_{\rm RVM,sm}
 :=\sum_{k\ge1}k^{-1/2}e^{i\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k\log k}
\end{equation}
converges. Consequently its path averages
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:smooth-clock-path-average}
 C_{\rm RVM,sm}^{\rm path}(N):=
 \sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{N-k+1}{N}
 k^{-1/2}e^{i\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k\log k}
\end{equation}
converge to the same limit $\mathscr D_{\rm RVM,sm}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put
\begin{equation*}
 \mathscr H_{\rm RVM,ph}(x):=\frac{\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}(x)\log x}{2\pi},\qquad
 \Psi_{\rm RVM,ph}(x):=2\pi\bigl(\mathscr H_{\rm RVM,ph}(x)-x\bigr).
\tag{S15.7}
\end{equation*}
Since $e^{2\pi i k}=1$, one has
$e^{i\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k\log k}=e^{i\Psi_{\rm RVM,ph}(k)}$.  By
Lemma~\CGref{lem:smooth-clock-phase-derivative},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:smooth-clock-phase-derivative}
 \mathscr H_{\rm RVM,ph}'(x)-1
 =\frac{\log\log x+1}{\log x}
 +O\!\left(\frac{(\log\log x)^2}{(\log x)^2}\right)>0
\end{equation}
for large $x$, and this difference is eventually decreasing. Hence on every dyadic block $X\le k\le2X$ the increments
$\Psi_{\rm RVM,ph}(k+1)-\Psi_{\rm RVM,ph}(k)$ are eventually positive, monotone,
and comparable to $\log\log X/\log X$. The elementary first-difference
estimate for exponential sums therefore gives, uniformly for
$X\le Y\le2X$,
\begin{equation*}
 \left|\sum_{X<k\le Y}e^{i\Psi_{\rm RVM,ph}(k)}\right|
 \ll\frac{\log X}{\log\log X}.
\tag{S15.8}
\end{equation*}
For completeness, this estimate follows by writing
$e^{i\Psi_{\rm RVM,ph}(k)}=(e^{i\Delta_k}-1)^{-1}
(e^{i\Psi_{\rm RVM,ph}(k+1)}-e^{i\Psi_{\rm RVM,ph}(k)})$, where
$\Delta_k=\Psi_{\rm RVM,ph}(k+1)-\Psi_{\rm RVM,ph}(k)$, and applying summation
by parts; monotonicity of $\Delta_k$ makes the total variation
$O(1/\min|\Delta_k|)$. Partial summation now yields
\begin{equation*}
 \sum_{X<k\le2X}k^{-1/2}e^{i\Psi_{\rm RVM,ph}(k)}
 \ll \frac{\log X}{\sqrt X\,\log\log X}.
\tag{S15.9}
\end{equation*}
The right side is summable over dyadic $X$, proving convergence of
\CGeqref{eq:smooth-clock-series}. The assertion for
\CGeqref{eq:smooth-clock-path-average} is then the Ces\`aro convergence of
the partial sums of a convergent series.
\end{proof}

The next finite construction shows why neither the zero-counting law nor
pointwise proximity to its smooth inverse can be substituted for control of
the phase displacement.

\begin{proposition}[A near-clock sequence with coherent diagonal phases]
\label{prop:rounded-clock-obstruction}
For sufficiently large integers $k$, put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:rounded-clock-definition}
 m_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}:=\operatorname{round}\!\bigl(\mathscr H_{\rm RVM,ph}(k)\bigr),
 \qquad
 \widetilde\gamma_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}:=\frac{2\pi m_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}}{\log k}.
\end{equation}
After altering finitely many initial terms if necessary, the sequence
$(\widetilde\gamma_k^{\rm RVM,rnd})$ is strictly increasing and satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:rounded-clock-closeness}
 |\widetilde\gamma_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}-\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k|\le\frac{\pi}{\log k},
 \qquad
 N_{\rm RVM,rnd}(T)-\mathcal N_{\rm RVM,sm}(T)=O(1),
\end{equation}
where $N_{\rm RVM,rnd}$ is its counting function. Nevertheless
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:rounded-clock-coherence}
 e^{i\widetilde\gamma_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}\log k}=1,
\end{equation}
and therefore
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:rounded-clock-growth}
 \sum_{k\le N}k^{-1/2}e^{i\widetilde\gamma_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}\log k}
 \sim2\sqrt N,\qquad
 \sum_{k\le N}\frac{N-k+1}{N}k^{-1/2}
 e^{i\widetilde\gamma_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}\log k}
 \sim\frac43\sqrt N.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The first inequality in \CGeqref{eq:rounded-clock-closeness} is immediate
from $|m_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}-\mathscr H_{\rm RVM,ph}(k)|\le1/2$. By
\CGeqref{eq:smooth-clock-phase-derivative},
$\mathscr H_{\rm RVM,ph}(k+1)-\mathscr H_{\rm RVM,ph}(k)>1$ eventually, so
$m_{k+1}^{\rm rnd}\ge m_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}+1$. Since $m_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}\asymp k$, one has
$m_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}\log(1+1/k)=O(1)<\log k$ eventually.  Together with
$m_{k+1}^{\rm rnd}-m_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}\ge1$, this proves
$m_{k+1}^{\rm rnd}/\log(k+1)>m_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}/\log k$ for all sufficiently
large $k$, hence $\widetilde\gamma_{k+1}>\widetilde\gamma_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}$.
Furthermore
\begin{equation*}
 \bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_{k+1}-\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k
 =\frac{2\pi}{\log k}(1+o(1)).
\tag{S15.10}
\end{equation*}
Hence, for all sufficiently large $k$, the first inequality in
\CGeqref{eq:rounded-clock-closeness} implies
$\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_{k-1}<\widetilde\gamma_k^{\rm RVM,rnd}<\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_{k+1}$. Thus the
counting function of the rounded sequence differs by at most a bounded
number of indices from the discrete smooth-clock count; since
$\mathcal N_{\rm RVM,sm}(\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k)=k$ and
$\mathcal N_{\rm RVM,sm}$ is increasing, this gives
$N_{\rm RVM,rnd}(T)-\mathcal N_{\rm RVM,sm}(T)=O(1)$.
Equation \CGeqref{eq:rounded-clock-coherence} follows directly from the
definition, and \CGeqref{eq:rounded-clock-growth} follows from
$\sum_{k\le N}k^{-1/2}\sim2\sqrt N$ and
$N^{-1}\sum_{k\le N}(N-k+1)k^{-1/2}\sim\frac43\sqrt N$.
\end{proof}

Thus even an increasing artificial sequence with the same smooth counting
law up to $O(1)$ and with individual displacement only $O(1/\log k)$ can
make the diagonal completely coherent. This is a deterministic obstruction,
not a statement about the zeros of $\zeta$.

For the actual ordinates, define the exact remainder
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:real-clock-remainder}
 \mathscr R_{\rm RVM,ph}(N):=
 \sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{N-k+1}{N}k^{-1/2}
 e^{i\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k\log k}\bigl(\chi_k^{\rm RVM,ph}-1\bigr).
\end{equation}
Then, identically,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:real-vs-smooth-path}
 C_{1/2}^{\rm path}(N)=C_{\rm RVM,sm}^{\rm path}(N)+\mathscr R_{\rm RVM,ph}(N).
\end{equation}
Since Proposition~\CGref{prop:smooth-clock-convergence} proves convergence
of the smooth term, the path average for the genuine ordinates converges if
and only if $\mathscr R_{\rm RVM,ph}(N)$ has a finite limit. No such estimate is
proved here. In particular, there is no basis here for asserting that the
limit of \CGeqref{eq:real-clock-remainder} is zero, or that the observed
finite value $-2.461+2.493i$ of $C_{1/2}^{\rm path}$ is its limiting value.
The unresolved problem is therefore the collective phase control of
$\chi_k^{\rm RVM,ph}$, not the smooth zero-counting law by itself.

\subsection{A scale benchmark and what it does not test}
\label{subsec:diagonal-null-benchmark}

For scale comparison only, replace the phases for $k\ge2$ by independent
uniform unit-circle variables, retaining the deterministic first term. The
exact second moments are
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:diagonal-null-moments}
 \mathbb E|\mathscr D_{1/2}(N)|^2=\sum_{k\le N}\frac1k,\qquad
 \mathbb E|C_{1/2}^{\rm path}(N)|^2
 =\sum_{k\le N}\frac{(N-k+1)^2}{N^2k}.
\end{equation}
At the stated $N$, the corresponding root-mean-square scales are
approximately $3.884$ and $3.686$. The observed
$|\mathscr D_{1/2}(N)|\approx3.11$ and
$|C_{1/2}^{\rm path}(N)|\approx3.50$ are therefore on this scale.

This independent-phase model is only a scale benchmark. It deliberately
destroys the intrinsic ordinal relation between $k$ and the zero-counting rank at $\gamma_k$ and therefore
cannot be used to conclude that the actual diagonal contains no arithmetic
structure. Likewise, unrestricted permutations of the actual ordinates
preserve their multiset but destroy the same ordinal relation; they answer a
more limited question about the importance of that pairing. The smooth-clock
decomposition \CGeqref{eq:diagonal-smooth-fluctuation-factorization} is the
more intrinsic comparison: it preserves the zero-counting law and isolates
the fluctuations that remain to be controlled.

The distinction can be summarized as follows. A fixed column is a Dirichlet
partial sum whose renormalized constant term detects a zeta zero. A fixed row
is a Landau zero-sum that detects prime powers (and whose pure-phase form uses
RH). The diagonal is not arbitrary, because its two coordinates are joined
by the zero-counting index, but no analogous arithmetic evaluation is proved
here. The smooth diagonal converges; an artificial sequence within
$O(1/\log k)$ of that clock can nevertheless be made fully coherent; and the
convergence of the path average for the genuine ordinates is reduced exactly
to the remainder \CGeqref{eq:real-clock-remainder}.

\section{Numerical data}
\label{sec:data}

The following census covers all $n\le N$, where $N=455\,052\,511$; it
contains $87\,385$ excursions and records $(u_\nu,\tau_\nu,v_\nu,H_\nu,\sigma_\nu)$ and the
curves $\Ap,\Am,\Anet$:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:census_values}
  \Ap(N)\approx6.3449\times10^9,\quad \Am(N)\approx-7.0877\times10^9,\quad
  \Anet(N)\approx-7.4279\times10^8,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:census_max}
  \max_\nu\frac{H_\nu}{\sqrt{\ell_\nu}}\approx2.0743,\qquad
  \max_\nu\frac{\mathfrak b_\nu}{\ell_\nu^{3/2}}\approx1.0214 .
\end{equation}
Since $|M(x)|\le0.571\sqrt x$ for $33\le x\le10^{16}$ \cite{Hurst18}, and
$|M(n)|\le\sqrt n$ for $n<33$ with equality only at $n=1$, the
constant $C_0=1$ is admissible in Lemma~\CGref{lem:transfer}, which gives
$\Ap(N)+|\Am(N)|\le\frac45N^{5/4}\approx5.317\times10^{10}$ against the observed
$\approx1.3433\times10^{10}$, a ratio of approximately $3.96$. Heuristic~\CGref{heu:renewal} with
$L=\sqrt N$ predicts $\#\{\ell_\nu>\sqrt N\}\asymp N^{1/4}\approx146$. At
this range $N^{0.05}\approx2.7$, so factors $x^\eps$ are not separable from
constants. These numerical values are computational observations and are not used as
premises of any theorem.  The v52 audit package contains
\texttt{COMPUTATIONAL\_PROVENANCE\_v52.txt} and
\texttt{MANIFEST\_SHA256.txt}, which identify the available regeneration
scripts and figures by SHA-256 checksum.  Reproducing the full census also
requires the original Möbius data file; where a raw input file is not
present in the audit package, the corresponding number remains explicitly
non-reproducible from the manuscript alone.  The conventions are those of
Proposition~\CGref{prop:explicit} and Definition~\CGref{def:Ms-terminal}.

\section{The RVM arithmetic system and its relation to the classical one}
\label{sec:RVM-pole-transport}

This section collects, in one namespace, the deterministic arithmetic system
obtained from the midpoint Riemann--von Mangoldt clock and distinguishes it
from the genuine zeta/Mertens system.  There are two RVM Mertens objects and
they must not be conflated.  The \emph{critical RVM baseline}
$M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}$ is built only from the synthetic ordinates
$\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$ and has its nontrivial spectral divisor on
$\Re s=1/2$ by construction.  The \emph{ordinate-transported object}
$M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ keeps the genuine real parts $\beta_k$ and replaces
only the genuine ordinates $\gamma_k$ by $\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$.  Its pole
abscissa is therefore the genuine quantity $\Theta$.

The same separation is used for the associated canonical products:
$P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}$ is the purely synthetic critical product, whereas
$P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ is the true-$\beta$ rigid-ordinate product.  The
first system has its own $\zeta_{\rm RVM}$, prime-power counting function
$J_{\rm RVM}$, prime counting function $\pi_{\rm RVM}$ and integer RVM-prime
realizations.  The second system is an analytic transport of the true zeta
divisor.  The section first develops the pure RVM system, then states the
exact defects relative to $M$, $\zeta$, the ordinary primes and $\pi$, and
only afterwards returns to the true-$\beta$ ordinate transport already used
in the previous version.

\subsection{The midpoint Riemann--von Mangoldt clock}

Let the nontrivial zeros in the upper half-plane, counted with multiplicity,
be
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-true-zero-enumeration}
 \rho_k=\beta_k+i\gamma_k,
 \qquad 0<\beta_k<1,
 \qquad 0<\gamma_1\le\gamma_2\le\cdots .
\end{equation}
$\beta_k\in(0,1)\subset\mathbb R$, $\gamma_k\in(0,\infty)$ and
$k\in\mathbb N$.  No critical-line assumption is made.  When two zeros belong to the same
functional pair at a common positive
ordinate, we fix the otherwise immaterial tie by placing
$1/2+d$ immediately before $1/2-d$, with $d>0$.  Occurrences on the
critical line retain their multiplicities.  This convention does not change
$P_\zeta$; it only fixes which consecutive rigid ordinates receive the two
members of an off-line pair.

Put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-midpoint-clock}
 \mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(T)
 :=\frac{T}{2\pi}\log\frac{T}{2\pi e}+\frac78,
 \qquad
 \mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})=k-\frac12
 \quad(k\in\mathbb N,\ \gamma_k^{\rm RVM}>2\pi).
\end{equation}
Solving with the principal branch of the Lambert function gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-midpoint-Lambert}
 \boxed{
 \gamma_k^{\rm RVM}
 =\frac{2\pi(k-11/8)}{W_0((k-11/8)/e)} .}
\end{equation}
The quotient in \CGeqref{eq:RVM-midpoint-Lambert} is positive also for
$k=1$.  This is the midpoint convention used by the file \texttt{zLamb.bin}.
It differs from the clock $\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k$ of
Section~\CGref{sec:diagonal-zero-phases}, where
$\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(\bar\gamma^{\rm RVM}_k)=k$.

\begin{lemma}[Bounded vertical displacement]
\label{lem:RVM-bounded-displacement}
One has
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-bounded-displacement}
 \gamma_k-\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}=O(1).
\end{equation}
The assertion is unconditional and includes multiplicities.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The Riemann--von Mangoldt formula gives
\begin{equation}
 N_\zeta(T)=\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(T)+O(\log(2+T)).
\end{equation}
At an ordinate $\gamma_k$, either endpoint convention for $N_\zeta$ gives
$N_\zeta(\gamma_k)=k+O(m_k)$, where $m_k$ is the multiplicity of the zero at
that ordinate.  The same counting formula gives $m_k=O(\log(2+\gamma_k))$.
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(\gamma_k)
 =k+O(\log(2+\gamma_k)).
\end{equation}
On the other hand,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal N_{\rm RVM}'(T)=\frac1{2\pi}\log\frac{T}{2\pi}\asymp\log T
\end{equation}
for large $T$.  The mean-value theorem applied between $\gamma_k$ and
$\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$ therefore proves
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-bounded-displacement}; finitely many initial ordinates are
absorbed into the constant.
\end{proof}


\subsection{The critical RVM zeta function}
\label{subsec:RVM-critical-zeta}

The midpoint clock itself defines a complete synthetic zeta system without
using the genuine real parts $\beta_k$.  Put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-critical-zeros}
 \rho_k^{\rm RVM}:=\frac12+i\gamma_k^{\rm RVM},
 \qquad
 \overline{\rho_k^{\rm RVM}}=\frac12-i\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}.
\end{equation}
The real part $1/2$ in \CGeqref{eq:RVM-critical-zeros} is an input of the
synthetic model; it is not a conclusion about the genuine zeta zeros.
Define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-critical-product}
 P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}(s)
 :=\prod_{k\ge1}
 \left(1+\frac{s(s-1)}{1/4+(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^2}\right),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-critical-xi-zeta}
 \xi_{\rm RVM}(s):=\frac12P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}(s),
 \qquad
 \zeta_{\rm RVM}(s):=\frac{P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}(s)}{G(s)},
 \qquad
 G(s):=s(s-1)\Gamma(s/2)\pi^{-s/2}.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Canonical characterization of $\zeta_{\rm RVM}$]
\label{thm:RVM-critical-zeta-characterization}
The product \CGeqref{eq:RVM-critical-product} converges absolutely and
locally uniformly.  The function $P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}$ is entire,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-critical-symmetry}
 P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}(s)=P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}(1-s),
\end{equation}
and its zeros, with multiplicity, are exactly
$\rho_k^{\rm RVM}$ and $\overline{\rho_k^{\rm RVM}}$.  Consequently
$\zeta_{\rm RVM}$ has a simple pole at $s=1$ of residue $1$, the usual
trivial zeros at the negative even integers, and nontrivial zeros exactly at
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-zeta-nontrivial-zeros}
 s=\frac12\mathbin{\pm}i\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}.
\end{equation}
Moreover $\xi_{\rm RVM}(s)=\xi_{\rm RVM}(1-s)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The clock law gives $\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}\asymp k/\log k$, hence
$\sum_k(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^{-2}<\infty$.  Each paired factor in
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-critical-product} differs from $1$ by
$O_K((\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^{-2})$ on a compact set $K$, proving normal
convergence.  The factor depends only on $s(s-1)$, which proves
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-critical-symmetry}.  The zero statement follows factor by
factor.  Finally $P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}(1)=1$ and
$G(s)=(s-1)(1+O(s-1))$ near $s=1$, while the poles of $\Gamma(s/2)$
produce the trivial zeros after division by $G$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Synthetic criticality is not classical RH]
\label{rem:RVM-criticality-input}
The zero line in \CGeqref{eq:RVM-zeta-nontrivial-zeros} is fixed by
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-critical-zeros}.  Thus any square-root exponent proved for
the pure RVM system is a theorem about this synthetic system; it cannot be
used as evidence that the genuine real parts $\beta_k$ equal $1/2$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The RVM prime-power count and prime count}
\label{subsec:RVM-prime-counting}

For $x>1$ define the symmetric RVM explicit-formula value
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-J-definition}
 J_{\rm RVM}(x)
 :=\li(x)
 -2\Re\sum_{k\ge1}
   \operatorname{Ei}\!\left(\rho_k^{\rm RVM}\log x\right)
 -\log2
 +\int_x^\infty\frac{dt}{t(t^2-1)\log t},
\end{equation}
where the zero sum is taken in the symmetric limiting sense supplied by the
clock ordering.  Put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-J-star}
 J_{\rm RVM}^{*}(x):=
 \begin{cases}J_{\rm RVM}(x),&x\ge2,\\0,&0<x<2.\end{cases}
\end{equation}
For comparisons that use only integer samples, also define the distinct
right-continuous step extension
\begin{equation*}
 J_{\rm RVM}^{*,\mathbb Z}(x)
 :=J_{\rm RVM}^{*}(\lfloor x\rfloor),
 \qquad x\in[1,\infty).
\tag{S16.1}
\end{equation*}
The symbols $J_{\rm RVM}^{*}$ and $J_{\rm RVM}^{*,\mathbb Z}$ are not
interchangeable: the first retains the continuous variation inside a unit
cell, whereas the second moves that entire variation to the next integer.
Set $\pi_{\rm RVM}(x)=0$ for $0<x<2$.  For $x\ge2$, the RVM prime count
is the exact finite Möbius inversion
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-pi-definition}
 \boxed{
 \pi_{\rm RVM}(x)
 :=\sum_{1\le m\le\log_2 x}
 \frac{\mu(m)}{m}J_{\rm RVM}^{*}(x^{1/m})
 \qquad(x\ge2).}
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Exact RVM prime-power inversion]
\label{prop:RVM-prime-power-inversion}
For every $x\ge2$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-J-pi-inversion}
 \boxed{
 J_{\rm RVM}^{*}(x)
 =\sum_{1\le m\le\log_2x}\frac1m
   \pi_{\rm RVM}(x^{1/m}).}
\end{equation}
Thus $J_{\rm RVM}$ and $\pi_{\rm RVM}$ determine one another exactly once
the support convention \CGeqref{eq:RVM-J-star} is fixed.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
This is ordinary Möbius inversion in the exponent variable.  Both sums are
finite because $x^{1/m}<2$ for $m>\log_2x$.
\end{proof}

\begin{hypothesis}[Lambert-clock square-root estimate]
\label{hyp:RVM-J-baseline}
The compact-uniform existence of the symmetric $\operatorname{Ei}$ sum is
proved in Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-path-J0-uniform}.  The additional analytic
hypothesis here is only the quantitative statement: the Lambert-clock
exponential sums satisfy the uniform blockwise van der Corput bounds,
endpoint estimates and tail bounds needed to give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-J-baseline}
 J_{\rm RVM}(x)-\li(x)
 =O\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{x}(\log\log x)^2}{\log x}\right).
\end{equation}
Under this hypothesis, finite M\"obius inversion gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-pi-baseline}
 \pi_{\rm RVM}(x)-\li(x)
 =O\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{x}(\log\log x)^2}{\log x}\right).
\end{equation}
\end{hypothesis}

\begin{remark}[Analytic proof obligation]
\label{rem:RVM-J-baseline-proof-obligation}
The stationary-phase calculation identifies the expected square-root scale,
but it does not by itself prove Hypothesis~\CGref{hyp:RVM-J-baseline}.  A
complete proof must control the symmetric meaning and branches of
$\operatorname{Ei}$, uniform tails, endpoint terms under integration by
parts, and the van der Corput constants uniformly over the Lambert-clock
blocks.  No later unconditional theorem uses
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-J-baseline}--\CGeqref{eq:RVM-pi-baseline}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[What the exponent means]
\label{rem:RVM-baseline-not-RH}
The factor $\sqrt{x}$ in Hypothesis~\CGref{hyp:RVM-J-baseline} comes from the
input $\Re\rho_k^{\rm RVM}=1/2$.  The hypothesis asks that the deterministic
clock should not worsen the inserted critical exponent.  It is not a deduction
of classical RH.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Integer RVM primes: weighted and $0/1$ realizations}
\label{subsec:RVM-integer-primes}

Define the integer RVM-prime weight by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-cn-definition}
 c_n^{\rm RVM}
 :=\pi_{\rm RVM}(n)-\pi_{\rm RVM}(n-1),
 \qquad n\ge2,
\end{equation}
and the corresponding integer-supported Euler object, initially for
$\Re s>1$, by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-weighted-zeta}
 \zeta_{\mathbb Z,\rm RVM}(s)
 :=\prod_{n\ge2}(1-n^{-s})^{-c_n^{\rm RVM}}.
\end{equation}
The associated prime-power count is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-JZ-definition}
 J_{\mathbb Z,\rm RVM}(x)
 :=\sum_{1\le m\le\log_2x}\frac1m
 \pi_{\rm RVM}(\lfloor x^{1/m}\rfloor).
\end{equation}
For real $x\ge1$ write
\begin{equation*}
 \Delta_{\rm RVM}^{\rm cont}(x)
 :=J_{\mathbb Z,\rm RVM}(x)-J_{\rm RVM}^{*}(x),
 \qquad
 \Delta_{\rm RVM}^{\rm step}(x)
 :=J_{\mathbb Z,\rm RVM}(x)-J_{\rm RVM}^{*,\mathbb Z}(x).
\tag{S16.2}
\end{equation*}
Both defects are real-valued.  A Stieltjes variation hypothesis must name
one of them; specifying values only on $\mathbb N$ does not by itself define
the measure $d\Delta$ on $[1,\infty)$.

\begin{hypothesis}[Uniform unit-cell control]
\label{hyp:RVM-unit-cell-control}
For $x$ sufficiently large and $0<h\le1$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-J-unit-cell}
 |J_{\rm RVM}(x+h)-J_{\rm RVM}(x)|
 \ll h+\frac{\log x}{\sqrt{x}},
\end{equation}
uniformly in the unit interval.  Consequently the finite inversion would
give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-pi-floor-control}
 \pi_{\rm RVM}(y)-\pi_{\rm RVM}(\lfloor y\rfloor)=O(1),
 \qquad c_n^{\rm RVM}=O(1).
\end{equation}
\end{hypothesis}

\begin{remark}[Why the unit-cell estimate is separate]
The global square-root estimate does not imply
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-J-unit-cell}.  Proving this stronger statement requires a
uniform estimate for increments of the truncated $\operatorname{Ei}$ sum,
with the high-clock tail and all endpoint terms controlled uniformly for
$0<h\le1$.  It is therefore kept as an explicit hypothesis rather than
being inferred from Hypothesis~\CGref{hyp:RVM-J-baseline}.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Conditional integer discretization of the zero divisor]
\label{cor:RVM-weighted-zero-divisor}
Assume Hypothesis~\CGref{hyp:RVM-unit-cell-control} and the Mellin--Stieltjes identification used in the proof below.
One has
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-JZ-difference}
 J_{\mathbb Z,\rm RVM}(x)-J_{\rm RVM}^{*}(x)=O(\log\log x).
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-weighted-factorization}
 \boxed{
 \zeta_{\mathbb Z,\rm RVM}(s)
 =\zeta_{\rm RVM}(s)e^{h_{\rm RVM}(s)},}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-h-definition}
 h_{\rm RVM}(s)
 :=s\int_1^\infty
 \bigl(J_{\mathbb Z,\rm RVM}(x)-J_{\rm RVM}^{*}(x)\bigr)x^{-s-1}\,dx
\end{equation}
is holomorphic for $\Re s>0$.  Hence the weighted integer system and
$\zeta_{\rm RVM}$ have the same zero divisor in $\Re s>0$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Under the unit-cell hypothesis, each summand in
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-JZ-definition} changes by $O(1/m)$ under the unit-cell
replacement, and $\sum_{m\le\log_2x}m^{-1}=O(\log\log x)$.
The Mellin--Stieltjes logarithm of an Euler product converts the difference
of prime-power counts into \CGeqref{eq:RVM-h-definition}; the logarithmic
growth in \CGeqref{eq:RVM-JZ-difference} makes the integral locally normally
convergent for $\Re s>0$.  Exponentiation introduces no zeros.
\end{proof}

The factorization above uses the continuous defect
$\Delta_{\rm RVM}^{\rm cont}$.  If one assumes weighted total variation for
$\Delta_{\rm RVM}^{\rm step}$ instead, comparison with
$\zeta_{\rm RVM}$ additionally requires control of the grid correction
\begin{equation*}
 H_{\rm RVM}^{\rm grid}(s)
 :=s\int_1^\infty
 \bigl(J_{\rm RVM}^{*,\mathbb Z}(x)-J_{\rm RVM}^{*}(x)\bigr)x^{-s-1}\,dx
\tag{S16.3}
\end{equation*}
on the stated half-plane, or an equivalent direct Mellin--Stieltjes
identification.  This is part of hypothesis 3b in the integer-step reading;
it is not supplied merely by 3a.

\begin{remark}[Positivity is a separate theorem target]
\label{rem:RVM-weight-positivity}
Equation~\CGeqref{eq:RVM-weighted-zeta} is an analytic weighted Euler object.
It is a positive Beurling-type generalized-prime system only when the weights
$c_n^{\rm RVM}$ are nonnegative.  Boundedness in
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-pi-floor-control} does not imply positivity.  This is distinct from existence constructions of Beurling systems satisfying RH-type prime-counting bounds; see, for example, \cite{BrouckeDebruyneVindas2020}.
\end{remark}

For the Poisson expansion of the Lambert clock, let $j\ge1$ denote the
Poisson harmonic.  The stationary point, phase and leading amplitude are
\begin{align}
\label{eq:RVM-stationary-point}
 T_j&=2\pi x^{1/j},\\
\label{eq:RVM-stationary-phase}
 \Phi_j(T_j)&=2\pi jx^{1/j}-\frac{11\pi j}{4},\\
\label{eq:RVM-stationary-amplitude}
 \mathcal A_j(x)&=\frac{\log x}{j^{3/2}}x^{1/(2j)}.
\end{align}
For $j=1$ the induced derivative term is exactly
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-first-Poisson-harmonic}
 2\cos(2\pi x),
\end{equation}
so that on every integer cell
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-cell-cancellation}
 \int_{n-1}^{n}2\cos(2\pi x)\,dx=0.
\end{equation}
The next stationary term has scale $x^{-1/4}$.

\begin{hypothesis}[Uniform RVM cell remainder]
\label{hyp:RVM-cell-remainder}
The Poisson/stationary-phase remainder after removing the first harmonic
satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-cell-remainder}
 c_n^{\rm RVM}-\int_{n-1}^{n}
 \left(\frac1{\log x}+2\cos(2\pi x)\right)dx
 =o\!\left(\frac1{\log n}\right)
 \qquad(n\to\infty).
\end{equation}
This increment formulation includes any jump at an integer endpoint; it
does not presuppose differentiability or absolute continuity of
$\pi_{\rm RVM}$.
\end{hypothesis}

\begin{proposition}[Consequences of the cell remainder]
\label{prop:RVM-eventual-positive-weights}
Hypothesis~\CGref{hyp:RVM-cell-remainder} implies that, for all sufficiently
large $n$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-eventual-positive-weights}
 0<c_n^{\rm RVM}<1.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Integrating the main density over $[n-1,n]$ gives
$1/\log n+O(1/(n\log^2n))$.  The first Poisson harmonic vanishes exactly by
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-cell-cancellation}, and the assumed remainder is
$o(1/\log n)$.  Thus $c_n^{\rm RVM}\sim1/\log n$, which proves both
inequalities eventually.
\end{proof}

Assuming \CGeqref{eq:RVM-eventual-positive-weights}, define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-rounded-count}
 R_{\rm RVM}(n):=
 \left\lfloor\pi_{\rm RVM}(n)+\frac12\right\rfloor,
 \qquad
 d_n^{\rm RVM}:=R_{\rm RVM}(n)-R_{\rm RVM}(n-1).
\end{equation}
After changing finitely many initial values one obtains the deterministic
integer set
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-integer-prime-set}
 \mathcal P_{\rm RVM}:=\{n\ge2:d_n^{\rm RVM}=1\}.
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}[Conditional $0/1$ RVM prime realization]
\label{cor:RVM-integer-prime-realization}
Under Hypotheses~\CGref{hyp:RVM-cell-remainder} and
\CGref{hyp:RVM-unit-cell-control}, and the Mellin--Stieltjes identification
of Corollary~\CGref{cor:RVM-weighted-zero-divisor}, the set
$\mathcal P_{\rm RVM}$ can be chosen so that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-P-count}
 \pi_{\mathcal P_{\rm RVM}}(n)=\pi_{\rm RVM}(n)+O(1).
\end{equation}
Its Euler product differs from $\zeta_{\rm RVM}$ by an exponential of a
holomorphic function on $\Re s>0$ and by finitely many elementary factors.
Consequently its nontrivial zero divisor in $\Re s>0$ is exactly
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-zeta-nontrivial-zeros}.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{The critical RVM Mertens object}
\label{subsec:RVM-critical-Mertens}

Define the normalized Mellin transform of the critical RVM baseline by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Mcrit-transform}
 \widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}(s)
 :=\frac1{\zeta_{\rm RVM}(s)}.
\end{equation}
Whenever ordinary inversion is justified put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Mcrit-inverse}
 M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}(x)
 :=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}
 \widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}(s)\frac{x^s}{s}\,ds,
 \qquad c>1,
\end{equation}
and otherwise keep it as a formal inverse-Mellin symbol until an admissible
function or distribution space and an inversion theorem are specified.

\begin{proposition}[Spectral characterization of $M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}$]
\label{prop:RVM-Mcrit-poles}
The nontrivial poles of $\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}$ are exactly
$1/2\mathbin{\pm}i\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$.  Hence its nontrivial pole abscissa
is exactly $1/2$ by construction.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
This is immediate from Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-critical-zeta-characterization}
and \CGeqref{eq:RVM-Mcrit-transform}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Gaussian square-root bound for the pure RVM baseline, conditional on vertical growth]
\label{prop:RVM-Mcrit-Gaussian}
Fix $c>1$ and $0<\varepsilon<c-1/2$.  Assume that, uniformly for
$1/2+\varepsilon\le\sigma\le c$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Mcrit-subgaussian-growth}
 \frac1{\zeta_{\rm RVM}(\sigma+it)}
 =\exp\!\bigl(o(t^2)\bigr)
 \qquad(|t|\to\infty),
\end{equation}
with the same bound on the horizontal segments used in the contour
rectangle.  For
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Mcrit-Gaussian}
 M_{\rm RVM,G}^{\rm crit}(x)
 :=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}
 e^{s^2}\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}(s)\frac{x^s}{s}\,ds,
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Mcrit-Gaussian-bound}
 M_{\rm RVM,G}^{\rm crit}(x)=O_\varepsilon(x^{1/2+\varepsilon}).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
On $s=\sigma+it$ one has
$|e^{s^2}|=e^{\sigma^2-t^2}$.  Hence
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-Mcrit-subgaussian-growth} makes both vertical integrals
absolutely convergent and forces the two horizontal sides of a height-$T$
rectangle to tend to zero as $T\to\infty$.  The strip
$1/2+\varepsilon\le\Re s\le c$ contains no nontrivial pole of
$1/\zeta_{\rm RVM}$; the point $s=1$ is a zero, not a pole, of that
reciprocal, and no trivial pole is crossed in this strip.  Cauchy's theorem
therefore moves the contour to $\Re s=1/2+\varepsilon$.  On the shifted line
$x^{\Re s}=x^{1/2+\varepsilon}$, while the remaining integral is finite by
the same Gaussian domination, proving the stated bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Unsmoothed pointwise control is separate]
\label{rem:RVM-Mcrit-unsmoothed}
Proposition~\CGref{prop:RVM-Mcrit-Gaussian} is a regularized statement.
A pointwise square-root bound for the unsmoothed inverse
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-Mcrit-inverse} requires vertical-growth and Mellin multiplier
control; it is not asserted merely from the location of the synthetic poles.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Exact symmetric Hadamard normalization of the genuine completed factor}
\label{subsec:RVM-Hadamard-normalization}
Put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-completed-true-factor}
 P_\zeta(s):=2\xi(s)=G(s)\zeta(s).
\end{equation}
For the positive ordinates, counted with multiplicity, define the paired
product
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-raw-paired-zeta-product}
 P_\zeta^{\rm pair}(s):=
 \prod_{k\ge1}\left(1-\frac{s}{\rho_k}\right)
                 \left(1-\frac{s}{\overline\rho_k}\right).
\end{equation}
The pairing in \CGeqref{eq:RVM-raw-paired-zeta-product} is by conjugation,
while the full upper-half-plane multiset also contains the functional mirror
$1-\overline\rho_k$ of every $\rho_k$.

\begin{theorem}[The paired Hadamard product has no residual exponential gauge]
\label{thm:RVM-Hadamard-normalization}
The product \CGeqref{eq:RVM-raw-paired-zeta-product} converges absolutely and
locally uniformly and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Hadamard-exponential}
 \boxed{P_\zeta(s)=P_\zeta^{\rm pair}(s).}
\end{equation}
Thus the possible factor $e^{a+bs}$ in an abstract order-one Hadamard
factorization is forced to be identically $1$ by the functional symmetry and
the normalization at $s=0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $\rho=\beta+i\gamma$ with $0<\beta<1$,
\begin{equation*}
 \left(1-\frac{s}{\rho}\right)
 \left(1-\frac{s}{\bar\rho}\right)
 =1-\frac{2\beta s}{\beta^2+\gamma^2}
   +\frac{s^2}{\beta^2+\gamma^2}
 =1+O_K(\gamma^{-2})
\tag{S17.1}
\end{equation*}
uniformly on a fixed compact set $K$.  Since
$N_\zeta(T)=O(T\log T)$, one has
$\sum_{\gamma_k>0}\gamma_k^{-2}<\infty$, so the product converges
absolutely and locally uniformly.

Write
\begin{equation*}
 f_{\beta,\gamma}(s)
 :=\frac{(s-\beta)^2+\gamma^2}{\beta^2+\gamma^2}.
\tag{S17.2}
\end{equation*}
If $\beta\ne1/2$, functional symmetry supplies at the same positive
ordinate the mirror $1-\beta+i\gamma$.  A direct calculation gives
\begin{equation*}
 f_{\beta,\gamma}(1-s)f_{1-\beta,\gamma}(1-s)
 =f_{\beta,\gamma}(s)f_{1-\beta,\gamma}(s),
\tag{S17.3}
\end{equation*}
and for $\beta=1/2$ the single factor is already invariant.  Absolute
convergence permits grouping the factors in these functional pairs, hence
\begin{equation*}
 P_\zeta^{\rm pair}(1-s)=P_\zeta^{\rm pair}(s).
\tag{S17.4}
\end{equation*}
For $|s|\le R$, the factor bound above gives
$\log\max_{|s|\le R}|P_\zeta^{\rm pair}(s)|
\le\sum_k\log(1+(R^2+2R)/\gamma_k^2)=O(R\log R)$,
by partial summation using $N_\zeta(T)=O(T\log T)$.
Thus $P_\zeta^{\rm pair}$ has order at most one.
Both $P_\zeta$ and $P_\zeta^{\rm pair}$ are entire of order at most one and
have exactly the same zeros with multiplicity.  Hadamard factorization
therefore gives
$P_\zeta=e^{a+bs}P_\zeta^{\rm pair}$ for constants $a,b$.  At $s=0$ both
functions equal $1$, so $e^a=1$.  Applying $s\mapsto1-s$ and using the two
functional symmetries gives $e^{bs}=e^{b(1-s)}$ for every $s$, hence $b=0$.
Therefore the ratio is the constant $1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why this matters for the transport identities]
\label{rem:RVM-Hadamard-gauge-resolved}
The exponential ambiguity is not discarded by convention: it is eliminated
by Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-Hadamard-normalization}.  Consequently every
product formula below for $\mathscr Q_{\rm RVM}$ and
$\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ is literally exact; no hidden factor
$e^{bs}$ remains in a Mellin multiplier or logarithmic derivative.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Exact defects relative to $M$, $\zeta$, the ordinary primes and $\pi$}
\label{subsec:RVM-classical-defects}

Let
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-critical-quotient}
 \boxed{
 \mathscr Q_{\rm RVM}(s)
 :=\frac{P_\zeta(s)}{P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}(s)}
 =\frac{\zeta(s)}{\zeta_{\rm RVM}(s)}.}
\end{equation}
By Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-Hadamard-normalization} it has the exact convergent paired product representation
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-critical-quotient-product}
 \mathscr Q_{\rm RVM}(s)
 =\prod_{k\ge1}
 \frac{(1-s/\rho_k)(1-s/\overline\rho_k)}
 {1+s(s-1)/(1/4+(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^2)}.
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Exact transform relation between the true and critical RVM Mertens objects]
\label{prop:RVM-Mcrit-M-relation}
For $\Re s>1$ and by meromorphic continuation wherever both sides are
defined,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Mcrit-M-relation}
 \boxed{
 \widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}(s)
 =\mathscr Q_{\rm RVM}(s)\widehat M(s),
 \qquad
 \widehat M(s)=\frac1{\zeta(s)}.}
\end{equation}
If the inverse-Mellin distributions of $\mathscr Q_{\rm RVM}$ and of
$\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}/s$ exist in a common space, and convolution
with $M$ is defined there, write the multiplier kernel as
$\kappa_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}$.  Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Mcrit-convolution}
 M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}
 =\kappa_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}*_{\rm Mel}M
\end{equation}
as Mellin distributions.  Moreover
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Mcrit-difference}
 \widehat{(M-M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit})}(s)
 =\frac{1-\mathscr Q_{\rm RVM}(s)}{\zeta(s)}.
\end{equation}
Thus a genuine zero not cancelled by a coincidence with the RVM divisor
survives as a pole of the difference transform; ordinate closeness alone does
not imply a smaller pointwise exponent for $M-M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}$.
\end{proposition}

Define the true-prime/RVM-prime defect at the integers by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-prime-defect}
 \varepsilon_n^{\rm RVM}
 :=\mathbf1_{\{n\text{ prime}\}}-c_n^{\rm RVM},
 \quad(n\ge2),\qquad
 E_{\rm RVM}(N)
 :=\sum_{2\le n\le N}\varepsilon_n^{\rm RVM}.
\end{equation}
Then exactly
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-prime-defect-cumulative}
 \boxed{E_{\rm RVM}(N)=\pi(N)-\pi_{\rm RVM}(N).}
\end{equation}
For $\Re s>1$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-prime-defect-product}
 \frac{\zeta(s)}{\zeta_{\mathbb Z,\rm RVM}(s)}
 =\prod_{n\ge2}(1-n^{-s})^{-\varepsilon_n^{\rm RVM}},
\end{equation}
and with \CGeqref{eq:RVM-weighted-factorization},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-zeta-defect-factorization}
 \boxed{
 \frac{\zeta(s)}{\zeta_{\rm RVM}(s)}
 =e^{h_{\rm RVM}(s)}
  \prod_{n\ge2}(1-n^{-s})^{-\varepsilon_n^{\rm RVM}}}
 \qquad(\Re s>1).
\end{equation}
Thus the same discrepancy is visible either in the zero divisor through
$\mathscr Q_{\rm RVM}$ or on the integer-prime side through
$\varepsilon_n^{\rm RVM}$.

Let
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-clock-count}
 N_{\rm RVM}(T):=\#\{k:\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}\le T\}
 =\left\lfloor\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(T)+\frac12\right\rfloor
 \quad(T\ge2\pi),
\end{equation}
Writing the classical Riemann--von Mangoldt formula in the form
$N_\zeta(T)=\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(T)+S(T)+O(T^{-1})$ away from ordinates and
putting
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-quantization-sawtooth}
 \Psi_{\rm RVM}(T)
 :=\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(T)
 -\left\lfloor\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(T)+\frac12\right\rfloor,
\end{equation}
one obtains
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-count-defect}
 \boxed{
 N_\zeta(T)-N_{\rm RVM}(T)
 =S(T)+\Psi_{\rm RVM}(T)+O(T^{-1}).}
\end{equation}
The bounded sawtooth $\Psi_{\rm RVM}$ is the clock quantization error; the
arithmetical zero-count fluctuation is carried by $S(T)$.

\begin{remark}[First-order ordinate displacement]
\label{rem:RVM-first-order-displacement}
Linearizing the two midpoint equations gives, at first order,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-first-order-displacement}
 \gamma_k-\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}
 \approx-\frac{2\pi S(\gamma_k)}
 {\log(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}/2\pi)}.
\end{equation}
This formula is diagnostic, not an exact replacement for the zero-counting
identity \CGeqref{eq:RVM-count-defect}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The classical boundary]
\label{rem:RVM-von-Koch-boundary}
Because $\zeta_{\rm RVM}$ has no zeros in $\Re s>1/2$, asking that the true
quotient $\zeta/\zeta_{\rm RVM}$ be zero-free there is equivalent to asking
that $\zeta$ itself be zero-free there.  With the usual vertical-growth
hypotheses this is the classical von Koch/RH boundary.  The RVM construction
does not make that defect estimate easier by itself; its value is that the
synthetic baseline and the arithmetic defect are separated exactly.
\end{remark}


\subsection{A Hamburger endpoint criterion}
\label{subsec:RVM-Hamburger-endpoint}
Let the positive genuine ordinates $\gamma_k$ be counted with multiplicity and
define the critical-line projection
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:zeta-line-product}
 P_{\rm line}(s):=
 \prod_{k\ge1}\left(1+\frac{s(s-1)}{1/4+\gamma_k^2}\right),
 \qquad
 \zeta_{\rm line}(s):=\frac{P_{\rm line}(s)}{G(s)}.
\end{equation}
The product converges locally normally, satisfies
$P_{\rm line}(s)=P_{\rm line}(1-s)$, is normalized by
$P_{\rm line}(0)=P_{\rm line}(1)=1$, and has all nontrivial zeros on
$\Re s=1/2$ with exactly the genuine ordinates.  Hence
$\zeta_{\rm line}$ has the same gamma factor, functional equation and
residue $1$ at $s=1$ as $\zeta$.

Hamburger's converse theorem \cite{Hamburger1921} says, in the form used
here, that if a Dirichlet series $F(s)=\sum a_n n^{-s}$ is absolutely
convergent for $\Re s>1$, $(s-1)^mF(s)$ is entire of finite order for some
$m$, and $F$ satisfies the zeta functional equation, then
$F=c\zeta$; the residue normalization fixes $c=1$.  Therefore the single
endpoint condition
\begingroup
