\renewcommand{\theHequation}{RVM.D}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:zeta-line-D}
 \tag{D}
 \boxed{\zeta_{\rm line}(s)=\sum_{n\ge1}a_n n^{-s}
 \quad\text{absolutely for }\Re s>1}
\end{equation}
\endgroup
is equivalent to RH.  This is a Category~3 reformulation, not a proof: the
genuine ordinates have already been inserted into $\zeta_{\rm line}$.

Under the Hamburger hypotheses, condition~\CGeqref{eq:zeta-line-D} forces the
logarithmic prime-power measure of the endpoint to be the classical atomic
measure $\sum_{p,m}m^{-1}\delta_{p^m}$.  In contrast, the formal RVM
explicit-formula picture starts from a continuous clock density.  The
unresolved transition is therefore continuous-to-atomic.  The arithmetic
information enters through the genuine zero-count fluctuation $S(T)$.
Selberg's prime approximation to $S(T)$ is a mean statement, not pointwise
control at every $\gamma_k$; proving (D) by this route would require enough
pointwise control at those ordinates to produce only prime-power frequencies
and to exclude cross-frequencies such as $\log p_1\pm\log p_2$.  No such
estimate is proved here.

\subsection{A critical-line ordinate homotopy from the RVM clock to the genuine ordinates}
\label{subsec:RVM-critical-line-homotopy}

This subsection expands the critical-line path that was only summarized in
the preceding version.  It moves the ordinates and \emph{never} the real
part of a zero.  To remain consistent with the notation of this paper, the
$\tau_k$ of the separate working note are written here as
$\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$.  Put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-displacement}
 \delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line}:=\gamma_k-\gamma_k^{\rm RVM},
 \qquad
 \gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path}:=\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}+\kappa\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line},
 \qquad 0\le\kappa\le1.
\end{equation}
Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-bounded-displacement} gives a constant $D$ with
$|\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line}|\le D$ for every $k$ after enlarging $D$ to absorb finitely many
initial ordinates.

\begin{theorem}[Critical-line path and transported analytic class]
\label{thm:RVM-critical-line-path}
For $0\le\kappa\le1$ define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-product}
 P_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}(s):=\prod_{k\ge1}
 \left(1+\frac{s(s-1)}{1/4+(\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path})^2}\right),
 \qquad
 \zeta_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}(s):=\frac{P_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}(s)}{G(s)}.
\end{equation}
Then the product converges absolutely and locally uniformly jointly in
$(s,\kappa)$.  For every $\kappa$, $P_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}$ is entire of order one,
$P_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}(s)=P_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}(1-s)$, and $\zeta_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}$ has residue $1$ at $s=1$,
the usual trivial zeros, and nontrivial zeros exactly
\begin{equation}
 \frac12\mathbin{\pm}i\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path}.
\end{equation}
For $0\le\kappa<1$ these nontrivial zeros are simple; at $\kappa=1$ their
multiplicities are exactly the multiplicities of the genuine ordinates.
Moreover
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-endpoints}
 \boxed{\zeta_{\rm RVM,0}^{\rm path}=\zeta_{\rm RVM},\qquad
        \zeta_{\rm RVM,1}^{\rm path}=\zeta_{\rm line}.}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Because $\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path}$ lies between the two positive ordinates
$\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$ and $\gamma_k$, the bounded-displacement lemma and
$\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}\asymp k/\log k$ imply
$\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path}\asymp k/\log k$ uniformly in $\kappa$ for large $k$.  Hence
$\sum_k (\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path})^{-2}$ converges uniformly in $\kappa$.  On a compact
set $K\subset\mathbb C$ the $k$th factor differs from $1$ by
$O_K(t_k^{-2})$, so the Weierstrass criterion gives joint local normal
convergence.  The bound
$\sum_k\log(1+(R^2+R)/(1/4+(\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path})^2))=O(R\log R)$
gives order at most one, while the zero count
$\#\{k:\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path}\le T\}\asymp T\log T$ gives order at least one.
Hence the order is exactly one.  Dependence only on $s(s-1)$ gives the functional symmetry.
The assertions at $s=1$, at the negative even integers and at the nontrivial
zeros follow exactly as in
Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-critical-zeta-characterization}.

For $\kappa<1$,
\begin{equation*}
 \gamma_{k+1,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path}-\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path}
 =(1-\kappa)(\gamma_{k+1}^{\rm RVM}-\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})
 +\kappa(\gamma_{k+1}-\gamma_k)>0,
\tag{S17.5}
\end{equation*}
because the RVM clock is strictly increasing and the genuine ordinate list
is nondecreasing.  At $\kappa=1$ repeated genuine ordinates give precisely
their multiplicities.  The endpoint identities are the definitions of
$P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm crit}$ and $P_{\rm line}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Endpoint identification]
\label{thm:RVM-path-endpoint-identification}
With the path above,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-P}
 \boxed{\mathbf P:\ \zeta_{\rm RVM,1}^{\rm path}=\zeta
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \mathrm{RH}.}
\end{equation}
The symbol $\mathbf P$ is used below only when the endpoint is identified
with the classical zeta function; the construction of the path itself does
not assume it.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $\zeta_{\rm RVM,1}^{\rm path}=\zeta$, the nontrivial zeros of $\zeta$ are those of
$\zeta_{\rm RVM,1}^{\rm path}=\zeta_{\rm line}$ and therefore lie on $\Re s=1/2$.  Conversely,
assume RH.  Then the genuine paired product
$P_\zeta=P_\zeta^{\rm pair}$ from
Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-Hadamard-normalization} has factors
$1+s(s-1)/(1/4+\gamma_k^2)$, hence
$P_\zeta=P_{\rm line}$.  Division by the common factor $G$ gives
$\zeta=\zeta_{\rm line}=\zeta_{\rm RVM,1}^{\rm path}$.
\end{proof}

\noindent\textbf{Critical-line input versus endpoint identification.}\quad
Putting the synthetic zeros on $\Re s=1/2$ is a definition of the RVM path,
not an assumption that the genuine zeros lie there.  If RH is false, the
endpoint product $\zeta_{\rm line}$ still exists, but its zero divisor differs
from that of $\zeta$, so the path does not arrive at the classical zeta
function.  The mathematical target is therefore an independent proof of the
endpoint equality in $\mathbf P$.  Using that equality as a premise gives a
conditional consequence equivalent to RH; merely constructing the
critical-line path does not assume RH.

The next results move to the explicit-formula side.  Write $u=\log x>0$ and
put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-T}
 \mathcal T(t,u):=\Re\operatorname{Ei}((1/2+it)u),
 \qquad
 C_0(x):=-\log2+\int_x^\infty\frac{dv}{v(v^2-1)\log v}.
\end{equation}
Initially let $\varphi\in C_c^1((0,\infty);\mathbb R)$; extend all
paired identities complex-linearly to complex test functions.  Set
\begin{equation}
 g_\varphi(u):=e^{u/2}\varphi(u),
 \qquad
 Q_\varphi(t):=\int_0^\infty\mathcal T(t,u)\varphi(u)\,du.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Smoothing converts the ordinate derivative into a summable kernel]
\label{lem:RVM-path-Ei-smoothing}
One has
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-T-derivative}
 \partial_t\mathcal T(t,u)
 =\Re\frac{i e^{(1/2+it)u}}{1/2+it}
 =\sqrt{x}\,
 \frac{t\cos(tu)-\tfrac12\sin(tu)}{1/4+t^2}.
\end{equation}
Moreover
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-Qprime}
 Q_\varphi'(t)
 =\Re\frac{i\widehat g_\varphi(-t)}{1/2+it},
 \qquad
 |Q_\varphi'(t)|\le\frac{\|g_\varphi'\|_1}{t^2},
\end{equation}
and $Q_\varphi(t)=O_\varphi(t^{-2})$ as $t\to\infty$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $\operatorname{Ei}'(z)=e^z/z$, differentiation of
$z=(1/2+it)u$ gives the first identity.  Integrating against $\varphi$
gives the Fourier transform in \CGeqref{eq:RVM-path-Qprime}.  Integration
by parts yields
$|\widehat g_\varphi(-t)|\le\|g_\varphi'\|_1/t$, which proves the
$t^{-2}$ bound.  Finally, for the real part of the principal branch, uniformly on the
compact $u$-support of $\varphi$,
\begin{equation*}
 \mathcal T(t,u)=\frac{e^{u/2}}{u}\frac{\sin(tu)}{t}
 +O_\varphi(t^{-2}).
\tag{S17.6}
\end{equation*}
The possible imaginary constant in the asymptotic of
$\operatorname{Ei}$ does not contribute to $\mathcal T$.
Integration by parts in the leading term, with
$e^{u/2}\varphi(u)/u\in C_c^1((0,\infty))$, supplies one more factor
$t^{-1}$.  This proves $Q_\varphi(t)=O_\varphi(t^{-2})$.
In particular $\sum_k|Q_\varphi(\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path})|$ converges uniformly in
$\kappa$; the same estimates are uniform on bounded sets of test functions
with a common compact support.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Distributional transport on the prime-counting side]
\label{thm:RVM-path-distributional-J}
Define $J_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}$ as a distribution in $u=\log x$ by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-Jkappa}
 J_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}(x):=\li(x)-2\sum_{k\ge1}\mathcal T(\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path},\log x)+C_0(x),
\end{equation}
where the zero sum is interpreted after testing against
$C_c^1((0,\infty))$.  Then
$\kappa\mapsto J_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}$ is $C^1$ with values in
$\mathcal D'((0,\infty))$, and for every test function
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-Tonelli}
 \sum_{k\ge1}|\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line}|\int_0^1
 \left|\left\langle
 \partial_t\mathcal T(\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path},\cdot),\varphi
 \right\rangle\right|d\kappa<\infty.
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{align}
\label{eq:RVM-path-J-derivative}
 \partial_\kappa J_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}(x)
 &=-2\sqrt{x}\sum_{k\ge1}\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line}
 \frac{\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path}\cos(\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path}u)
       -\tfrac12\sin(\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path}u)}
      {1/4+(\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path})^2},\\
\label{eq:RVM-path-endpoint-distribution}
 J_{\rm RVM,1}^{\rm path}-J_{\rm RVM,0}^{\rm path}
 &=-2\sum_{k\ge1}
 \bigl(\mathcal T(\gamma_k,\cdot)
       -\mathcal T(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM},\cdot)\bigr)
 =\int_0^1\partial_\kappa J_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}\,d\kappa
\end{align}
in $\mathcal D'$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The last estimate in Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-path-Ei-smoothing} first
justifies the defining tested zero sum itself, absolutely and uniformly in
$\kappa$.  For every $k$, the fundamental theorem of calculus in $\kappa$ gives the
endpoint difference as the integral of
$\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line}\partial_t\mathcal T$.  By
Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-path-Ei-smoothing}, bounded displacement and
$\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path}\asymp\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$,
\begin{equation*}
 |\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line}|\int_0^1
 \left|\langle\partial_t\mathcal T(\gamma_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM,path},\cdot),\varphi\rangle\right|
 d\kappa
 \ll_\varphi (\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^{-2}.
\tag{S17.7}
\end{equation*}
The right-hand series converges.  Tonelli--Fubini therefore justifies the
interchange, and the same majorant gives continuous dependence on $\kappa$
and differentiation term by term in the distribution topology.
\end{proof}

The distributional theorem is deliberately stronger in rigor but weaker in
pointwise content than a formal interchange of the two infinite operations.
No pointwise convergence at an intermediate $0<\kappa<1$ is needed below.


\subsection*{Quadratic transport and the corrected logarithmic interpolation}
\phantomsection
\label{subsec:RVM-quadratic-transport}
For fixed $b>0$, the quadratic Taylor majorant converges at every fixed height; bounded displacement alone gives an $O_b(\log(t+2))$ bound, not a divergent series.  The proposed affine-geodesic argument has a valid transport core, but its
normalization term cannot be dropped.  Write
$\tau_k^{\rm RVM}:=\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$,
$\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line}:=\gamma_k-\tau_k^{\rm RVM}$, and
$\tau_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM}:=\tau_k^{\rm RVM}+\kappa\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line}$.
The first sequence is strictly increasing; the genuine ordinates are
nondecreasing and retain multiplicity.  All these quantities are real,
$k\in\mathbb N$, and $D_{\rm RVM}:=\sup_k|\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line}|<\infty$.
Counts are right-continuous.  A time at an endpoint is included on the
left and excluded on the right of a transport interval.

\begin{auditproposition}[Monotone transport and the quadratic displacement]
\label{prop:RVM-quadratic-transport}
Let $N_{\rm RVM}(t)=\#\{k:\tau_k^{\rm RVM}\le t\}$ and
$E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm tr}(t):=N_{\rm RVM}(t)-N_\zeta(t)$.
Let $\mathcal B_{\rm RVM}(t)$ contain the indices whose two endpoints
straddle $t$ under the preceding half-open convention.  At every $t$ all
these intervals have the same orientation, and their indices form exactly
the consecutive block between the two counts.  Moreover, for every finite
$K\in\mathbb N$,
\begin{equation*}
 \frac12\sum_{k\le K}(\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line})^2
 =\int_0^\infty\sum_{k\in\mathcal B_{\rm RVM}(t),\ k\le K}
                 |t-\tau_k^{\rm RVM}|\,dt.
\tag{QT.1}
\end{equation*}
For the midpoint RVM clock, unconditionally,
\begin{equation*}
 A_{\rm RVM}^{\rm tr}(T):=
 \sum_{\tau_k^{\rm RVM}\le T}(\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line})^2
 \sim\frac{T\log\log T}{\pi\log T}\qquad(T\to\infty).
\tag{QT.2}
\end{equation*}
In particular the stated upper bound follows without sampling $S$ at zeros.
\end{auditproposition}
\begin{proof}
If $\tau_k^{\rm RVM}\le t<\gamma_k$ and
$\gamma_j\le t<\tau_j^{\rm RVM}$, monotonicity forces both $j<k$ and $k<j$,
a contradiction.  The block is therefore
$\{N_\zeta(t)+1,\ldots,N_{\rm RVM}(t)\}$ for positive discrepancy and
the reversed-count block for negative discrepancy.  This includes repeated
genuine ordinates.  Integrating the distance over a single transport
interval gives $\tfrac12(\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line})^2$; finite additivity,
or Tonelli for the infinite identity in $[0,\infty]$, proves the formula.

Put $m=|E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm tr}(t)|$ and $h_{\rm RVM}^{\rm gap}(t)=2\pi/\log(t/2\pi)$ for large
$t$.  Every clock point in the block lies within $D_{\rm RVM}$ of $t$.
The adjacent clock point is at distance at most one clock gap.  The inverse
clock derivative gives, uniformly for gaps in this fixed-width neighborhood,
\begin{equation*}
 \tau_{j+1}^{\rm RVM}-\tau_j^{\rm RVM}
 =h_{\rm RVM}^{\rm gap}(t)\{1+O_{D_{\rm RVM}}(1/(t\log t))\}.
\tag{QT.3}
\end{equation*}
Summing the arithmetic progression of distances, with an arbitrary
fractional first gap, yields
\begin{equation*}
 H_{\rm RVM}^{\rm tr}(t):=\sum_{k\in\mathcal B_{\rm RVM}(t)}|t-\tau_k^{\rm RVM}|
 =\frac{\pi m^2}{\log(t/2\pi)}
 +O_{D_{\rm RVM}}\left(\frac m{\log t}+\frac{m^2}{t(\log t)^2}\right).
\tag{QT.4}
\end{equation*}
Here $m=O(\log t)$ by bounded displacement and the clock count.  The
Riemann--von Mangoldt formula gives
$E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm tr}(t)=-S(t)+O(1)$ almost everywhere; the sign is
immaterial for the square.  Selberg's unconditional second-moment input is
\begin{equation*}
 \int_0^T S(t)^2\,dt\sim\frac{T\log\log T}{2\pi^2}
 \quad\text{\cite[\S9.25, (9.25.1)]{Titchmarsh1986}}.
\tag{QT.5}
\end{equation*}
This uses the unconditional asymptotic, not an unverified $O(T)$ remainder.
Cauchy--Schwarz shows that replacing $S^2$ by $(E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm tr})^2$
changes the integral by $O(T\sqrt{\log\log T}+T)$.
Partial summation therefore gives
\begin{equation*}
 \int_{t_0}^{T}\frac{(E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm tr}(t))^2}{\log(t/2\pi)}\,dt
 \sim\frac{T\log\log T}{2\pi^2\log T},
\tag{QT.6}
\end{equation*}
for any fixed sufficiently large $t_0$.  The integrated $m/\log t$ error
is $O(T\sqrt{\log\log T}/\log T)$ by Cauchy--Schwarz on dyadic blocks;
the other error is $O(\log T)$ using $m=O(\log t)$.
Finally $\int_0^T H_{\rm RVM}^{\rm tr}$ differs from
$A_{\rm RVM}^{\rm tr}(T)/2$ only through intervals crossing $T$.
There are $O_{D_{\rm RVM}}(\log T)$ such clock indices, each contributing
$O(D_{\rm RVM}^2)$; the initial bounded range contributes $O(1)$.
These boundary errors are negligible.  The asymptotic follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{auditproposition}[Logarithmic interpolation with its normalization defect]
\label{prop:RVM-log-interpolation}
Fix a real $\sigma\in(1/2,1)$, put $s=\sigma+it$, $b=\sigma-1/2>0$,
and take $t>2D_{\rm RVM}+1$.  Choose logarithms coherently from the paired
products on the zero-free quadrant $1/2<\Re s<1$, $\Im s>0$; write
$L_{\rm RVM,\kappa}(s)=\log\zeta_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}(s)$.
Set $q_{\rm RVM}(u)=\log(u^2+1/4)$ for real $u>0$, and define
\begin{equation*}
 C_{\rm RVM}(\kappa):=\sum_{k\ge1}
 \left\{(1-\kappa)q_{\rm RVM}(\tau_k^{\rm RVM})
 +\kappa q_{\rm RVM}(\gamma_k)
 -q_{\rm RVM}(\tau_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM})\right\}.
\tag{QT.7}
\end{equation*}
This real series and its first two $\kappa$ derivatives converge uniformly.
It vanishes at the endpoints and is strictly negative for $0<\kappa<1$
if any displacement is nonzero.  There is a measurable set
$\mathcal Z_{\rm RVM,\sigma}\subset[1,\infty)$ with
\begin{equation*}
 \lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{\operatorname{meas}
 (\mathcal Z_{\rm RVM,\sigma}\cap[1,T])}{T}=0.
\tag{QT.8}
\end{equation*}
Outside this one set of times,
uniformly for $\kappa\in[0,1]$,
\begin{equation*}
 L_{\rm RVM,\kappa}(\sigma+it)
 =(1-\kappa)L_{\rm RVM,0}(\sigma+it)
 +\kappa L_{\rm RVM,1}(\sigma+it)+C_{\rm RVM}(\kappa)+o(1),
\tag{QT.9}
\end{equation*}
\begin{equation*}
 \partial_\kappa L_{\rm RVM,\kappa}(\sigma+it)
 =L_{\rm RVM,1}(\sigma+it)-L_{\rm RVM,0}(\sigma+it)
   +C_{\rm RVM}'(\kappa)+o(1).
\tag{QT.10}
\end{equation*}
Thus the renormalized logarithm $L_{\rm RVM,\kappa}-C_{\rm RVM}(\kappa)$
is asymptotically affine in density one.  The original normalized path is
not an exact logarithmic geodesic.
\end{auditproposition}
\begin{proof}
Put $z=s-1/2$ and
$g_{\rm RVM,s}(u)=\log(z^2+u^2)-q_{\rm RVM}(u)$, with the numerator
logarithm chosen continuously (its imaginary part is positive here).
Normal convergence of the paired product gives the exact master identity
\begin{equation*}
 L_{\rm RVM,\kappa}(s)-L_{\rm RVM,0}(s)
 =\sum_{k\ge1}\{g_{\rm RVM,s}(\tau_{k,\kappa}^{\rm RVM})
                   -g_{\rm RVM,s}(\tau_k^{\rm RVM})\},
 \qquad
 g_{\rm RVM,s}'(u)=\frac{2u(1/4-z^2)}{(z^2+u^2)(1/4+u^2)}.
\tag{QT.11}
\end{equation*}
In particular
$|z^2+u^2|=\sqrt{(u-t)^2+b^2}\sqrt{(u+t)^2+b^2}$, a product rather
than a single square root.  For $p_s(u)=\log(z^2+u^2)$, factoring the
numerator gives
\begin{equation*}
 |p_s''(u)|\le\frac1{(u-t)^2+b^2}+\frac1{(u+t)^2+b^2}.
\tag{QT.12}
\end{equation*}
Use the integral Taylor remainder, which is valid for complex-valued
functions; a real Lagrange point is not asserted.  The affine-interpolation
error for the numerator, and the error for its $\kappa$ derivative, are
bounded uniformly by a constant times
\begin{equation*}
 K_{\rm RVM,b}(t):=\sum_{k\ge1}(\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line})^2
 \left\{\frac1{((|\tau_k^{\rm RVM}-t|-D_{\rm RVM})_+)^2+b^2}
       +\frac1{(\tau_k^{\rm RVM}+t-D_{\rm RVM})^2+b^2}\right\}.
\tag{QT.13}
\end{equation*}
The denominator contribution is exactly $C_{\rm RVM}(\kappa)$.
Since $q_{\rm RVM}''(u)=2(1/4-u^2)/(u^2+1/4)^2<0$ for $u>1/2$,
and all endpoints exceed $1/2$, concavity gives the stated strict sign.
Uniform convergence and differentiation follow from the summable majorant
$O((\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line})^2/(\tau_k^{\rm RVM})^2)$.

For $\tau_k^{\rm RVM}\in[T/2,3T]$, the integral of the first kernel over
$[T,2T]$ is at most $2D_{\rm RVM}/b^2+\pi/b$.
Audit proposition~\CGref{prop:RVM-quadratic-transport} bounds the total
squared displacement there by $O(T\log\log T/\log T)$.
Outside this range, bounded displacement and the clock density bound the
averaged contribution by $O(\log T/T)$; the second kernel has the same
bound, by summing against $N_{\rm RVM}(u)=O(u\log(u+2))$.
Tonelli applies to these nonnegative majorants.  Hence
\begin{equation*}
 \frac1T\int_T^{2T}K_{\rm RVM,b}(t)\,dt
 \ll_{b,D_{\rm RVM}}\frac{\log\log T}{\log T}+\frac{\log T}{T}=o(1).
\tag{QT.14}
\end{equation*}
On dyadic blocks, Markov with a threshold equal to the square root of the
right-hand bound constructs a single exceptional set of density zero;
outside it $K_{\rm RVM,b}(t)\to0$.  This works for every $\kappa$
simultaneously.  The claimed expansions follow.
The proposed error with an additional
$\sum_k(\delta_k^{\rm RVM\to line})^2/(\tau_k^{\rm RVM})^2$ cannot tend
to zero: that sum is a fixed positive constant unless all displacements
vanish.  It is the normalization defect, not a disappearing tail.
\end{proof}

\begin{auditproposition}[Conditional loss of vertical rigidity]
\label{prop:RVM-rigidity-loss}
Assume the endpoint premise $\mathbf P$ (hence
$\zeta_{\rm RVM,1}^{\rm path}=\zeta$ and RH), and additionally a proved
vertical limit
$\log|\zeta_{\rm RVM}(\sigma+it)|\to c_{\rm RVM}$ for the chosen
$\sigma\in(1/2,1)$.  Then for every fixed $\kappa\in(0,1]$,
$\log|\zeta_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}(\sigma+it)|$ is unbounded above.
More precisely, in density one,
\begin{equation*}
 \log|\zeta_{\rm RVM,\kappa}^{\rm path}(\sigma+it)|
 =\kappa\log|\zeta(\sigma+it)|
 +(1-\kappa)c_{\rm RVM}+C_{\rm RVM}(\kappa)+o(1).
\tag{QT.15}
\end{equation*}
\end{auditproposition}
\begin{proof}
Take real parts in the preceding proposition.  Classical value-distribution
results, or the positive-lower-density form of Voronin universality, imply
that for each real $V$ the set of times with
$\log|\zeta(\sigma+it)|>V$ has positive lower density
\cite[Ch.~XI]{Titchmarsh1986}.  A density-zero exception cannot remove
that set.  Since $\kappa>0$, arbitrary $V$ prove unboundedness.
The fluctuation scale is multiplied by $\kappa$, with the deterministic
normalization shift explicitly retained.
\end{proof}

Consequently, \emph{if the vertical-limit premise is proved}, either RH is
false or this loss occurs at every positive path parameter.  Without that
premise the disjunction is not an unconditional consequence of this audit.
The conclusion obstructs propagation of a uniform vertical bound along the
path under these premises; it does not exclude every conceivable RH proof.
Route independence for the smoothed $J$ endpoint difference remains the
separate exact statement already proved above.  It does not turn the
normalized logarithmic path into an exact segment.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform convergence at the RVM endpoint]
\label{thm:RVM-path-J0-uniform}
The series
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k\ge1}\mathcal T(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM},u)
\end{equation}
converges uniformly for $u$ in every compact interval
$[a,b]\subset(0,\infty)$.  In particular the symmetric explicit-formula
object $J_{\rm RVM,0}^{\rm path}$ is an ordinary continuous function and equals the
$J_{\rm RVM}$ of \CGeqref{eq:RVM-J-definition}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $r_k^{\rm RVM}:=\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$ and
$\ell_k^{\rm RVM}:=\log(r_k^{\rm RVM}/2\pi)$.  The inverse-clock relation gives
\begin{equation*}
 r_k^{\rm RVM}\ell_k^{\rm RVM}\ge2\pi(k-11/8),\qquad
 \frac{2\pi}{\ell_{k+1}^{\rm RVM}}
 \le r_{k+1}^{\rm RVM}-r_k^{\rm RVM}\le\frac{2\pi}{\ell_k^{\rm RVM}},
\tag{S17.8}
\end{equation*}
and, by differentiating the inverse clock once more,
\begin{equation*}
 |r_{k+2}^{\rm RVM}-2r_{k+1}^{\rm RVM}+r_k^{\rm RVM}|
 \le\frac{4\pi^2}{r_k^{\rm RVM}(\ell_k^{\rm RVM})^3}.
\tag{S17.9}
\end{equation*}
For $E_k^{\rm RVM}=e^{iur_k^{\rm RVM}}$ and
$A_{K}^{\rm RVM}(u)=\sum_{k\le K}E_k^{\rm RVM}$, choose $k_0=k_0(b)$ so that
$u(r_{k+1}^{\rm RVM}-r_k^{\rm RVM})\le\pi$ for $u\in[a,b]$ and $k\ge k_0$.  Writing
$\Delta_k^{\rm RVM,path}=u(r_{k+1}^{\rm RVM}-r_k^{\rm RVM})$ and
\begin{equation*}
 E_k^{\rm RVM}=\frac{E_{k+1}^{\rm RVM}-E_k^{\rm RVM}}{e^{i\Delta_k^{\rm RVM,path}}-1}
\tag{S17.10}
\end{equation*}
and summing by parts gives, uniformly on $[a,b]$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-AK-log}
 |A_{K}^{\rm RVM}(u)|\ll_{a,b}1+\log K.
\end{equation}
Indeed the endpoint coefficient is $O_{a,b}(\log K)$ and the successive
coefficient differences are $O_{a,b}(1/k)$ by the displayed second-difference
bound.  A second Abel summation then yields uniform convergence of
$\sum e^{iur_k^{\rm RVM}}/r_k^{\rm RVM}$, because
$r_k^{\rm RVM}\asymp k/\log k$ and the resulting majorant is
$\sum \log^2k/k^2$.

Finally, uniformly for $u\in[a,b]$, the right-half-plane asymptotic of
$\operatorname{Ei}$ gives
\begin{equation*}
 \mathcal T(r_k^{\rm RVM},u)
 =\frac{e^{u/2}}{u}\frac{\sin(r_k^{\rm RVM}u)}{r_k^{\rm RVM}}+O_{a,b}((r_k^{\rm RVM})^{-2}).
\tag{S17.11}
\end{equation*}
The first series converges uniformly by the preceding paragraph and the
error series absolutely.  Hence the $\operatorname{Ei}$ series defining
$J_{\rm RVM,0}^{\rm path}$ converges uniformly on compact $u$-intervals.
\end{proof}

Accordingly, Hypothesis~\CGref{hyp:RVM-J-baseline} below concerns the
\emph{quantitative square-root estimate}; existence and compact-uniform
convergence of the RVM zero sum are already theorems.

For the endpoint $\kappa=1$, define
\begin{equation}
 B(T;u):=\sum_{0<\gamma\le T}e^{i\gamma u},
\end{equation}
where the genuine upper-half-plane zeros are counted with multiplicity.
Also define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-D}
 D(T;u):=\sum_{0<\gamma\le T}e^{i\gamma u}
 \left(\cosh((\beta-1/2)u)-1\right).
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Exact off-line defect]
\label{lem:RVM-path-offline-defect}
For every fixed $u>0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-B-defect}
 \boxed{
 B(T;u)=\sum_{0<\gamma\le T}x^{\rho-1/2}-D(T;u),
 \qquad x=e^u.}
\end{equation}
Moreover the standard Selberg zero-density estimate implies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-A2}
 \sum_{0<\gamma\le T}(\beta-1/2)^2\ll\frac{T}{\log T},
 \qquad
 |D(T;u)|\ll_u\frac{T}{\log T}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The upper-half-plane zero multiset is invariant under
$\beta+i\gamma\mapsto1-\beta+i\gamma$.  Averaging the two equal sums
$\sum e^{i\gamma u}e^{\pm(\beta-1/2)u}$ gives the hyperbolic cosine and
hence \CGeqref{eq:RVM-path-B-defect}.  For the second statement, the
standard Selberg estimate
$N(1/2+a,T)\ll T^{1-a/4}\log T$ gives, by layer-cake integration,
\begin{equation*}
 \sum_{\beta>1/2}(\beta-1/2)^2
 =\int_0^{1/2}2a\,N(1/2+a,T)\,da
 \ll\frac{T}{\log T};
\tag{S17.12}
\end{equation*}
the left half is identical by symmetry.  Finally
$\cosh y-1\le\tfrac12y^2\cosh|y|$ proves the bound for $D$.
\end{proof}

The Landau--Gonek explicit formula \cite{Gonek1993} gives, uniformly when
$x=e^u$ ranges over a compact subset of $(1,\infty)$ at positive distance
from every prime power,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-Landau-Gonek-local}
 \sum_{0<\gamma\le T}x^\rho=O_x(\log T).
\end{equation}
At a prime power it has the familiar linear main term
$-(T/2\pi)\Lambda(x)$.

\begin{theorem}[Pointwise endpoint criterion and the role of $\mathbf P$]
\label{thm:RVM-path-J1-convergence}
Let $x=e^u>1$ be not a prime power.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item Without $\mathbf P$, the series
$\sum_k e^{i\gamma_ku}/\gamma_k$ converges if and only if the finite limit
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-D-integral}
 \lim_{T\to\infty}\int_{14}^{T}\frac{D(t;u)}{t^2}\,dt
\end{equation}
exists.
\item Under $\mathbf P$ (equivalently RH), $D\equiv0$ and both
$\sum e^{i\gamma_ku}/\gamma_k$ and
$\sum\mathcal T(\gamma_k,u)$ converge uniformly when $x$ ranges over a
compact set at positive distance from the prime powers.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Partial summation gives
\begin{equation*}
 \sum_{\gamma_k\le T}\frac{e^{i\gamma_ku}}{\gamma_k}
 =\frac{B(T;u)}{T}+\int_{14}^{T}\frac{B(t;u)}{t^2}\,dt.
\tag{S17.13}
\end{equation*}
Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-path-offline-defect} implies $B(T;u)/T\to0$.
For non-prime-power $x$, \CGeqref{eq:RVM-path-Landau-Gonek-local} makes the
integral of the $x^{\rho-1/2}$ part absolutely convergent at infinity; by
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-path-B-defect}, the only remaining issue is exactly
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-path-D-integral}.  This proves (a).

Under $\mathbf P$, Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-path-endpoint-identification}
gives $\beta=1/2$ for every zero, so $D=0$.  The local Landau--Gonek bound is
uniform on compact sets separated from the prime powers, and partial
summation gives a uniformly Cauchy series.  The asymptotic
\begin{equation*}
 \mathcal T(\gamma,u)
 =\frac{e^{u/2}}{u}\frac{\sin(\gamma u)}{\gamma}+O_K(\gamma^{-2})
\tag{S17.14}
\end{equation*}
then gives the same conclusion for the $\operatorname{Ei}$ series.
\end{proof}

At a prime power, under $\mathbf P$, the real Landau--Gonek main term
makes $\sum e^{i\gamma_k u}/\gamma_k$ logarithmically divergent through its
cosine part.  This does not imply divergence of
$\sum\mathcal T(\gamma_k,u)$: its leading term involves the sine part.
The classical symmetric explicit formula assigns the midpoint value at a
prime-power jump.  The preceding uniform-convergence theorem is stated
away from prime powers; it makes no assertion of uniform convergence
across a jump.  Distributional statements remain available there.

\begin{theorem}[Identity between the two endpoints]
\label{thm:RVM-path-endpoint-identity}
Let $K\subset(1,\infty)$ be compact and separated from the prime powers.
Under $\mathbf P$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-endpoint-J}
 \boxed{
 J-J_{\rm RVM}
 =-2\sum_{k\ge1}
 \bigl(\mathcal T(\gamma_k,\log x)
       -\mathcal T(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM},\log x)\bigr)}
\end{equation}
pointwise and uniformly on $K$, and the same identity holds in
$\mathcal D'(U)$ on every open $U\subset(1,\infty)$ containing no prime power.  No pointwise statement at an intermediate
$0<\kappa<1$ is needed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The RVM endpoint converges uniformly by
Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-path-J0-uniform}, and the genuine-ordinate endpoint
converges uniformly under $\mathbf P$ by
Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-path-J1-convergence}.  Their difference is therefore
the pointwise series in \CGeqref{eq:RVM-path-endpoint-J}.  Under
$\mathbf P$, the classical explicit formula groups the genuine zeros as
$1/2\pm i\gamma_k$, so its zero term is exactly
$2\sum_k\mathcal T(\gamma_k,\log x)$ and hence $J_{\rm RVM,1}^{\rm path}=J$.  The
distributional identity follows independently from
Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-path-distributional-J} and uniqueness of the
distributional limit.
\end{proof}

The next identity does not use $\mathbf P$; it compares the two ordinate
sets as distributions.  Put
\begin{equation}
 N_{\rm RVM}(t):=\#\{k:\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}\le t\}
 =\left\lfloor\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(t)+\frac12\right\rfloor
 \qquad(t\ge2\pi),
\end{equation}
let $N_\zeta(t)$ be the genuine zero count, define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal H_\zeta(t):=1+\frac{\theta(t)}{\pi},
 \qquad
 S(t):=N_\zeta(t)-\mathcal H_\zeta(t),
 \qquad
 \mathfrak s(y):=\{y\}-\frac12,
\end{equation}
and recall from Stirling's expansion that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-clock-difference}
 \mathcal H_\zeta(t)-\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(t)
 =\frac{1}{48\pi t}+O(t^{-3}).
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Exact route-independent endpoint formula]
\label{prop:RVM-path-route-independent}
For every $\varphi\in C_c^1((0,\infty))$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-route-independent}
 \boxed{
 \langle J_{\rm RVM,1}^{\rm path}-J_{\rm RVM,0}^{\rm path},\varphi\rangle
 =2\int_{10}^{\infty}Q_\varphi'(t)
 \left[
 S(t)+\mathfrak s\!\left(\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(t)+\frac12\right)
 +\mathcal H_\zeta(t)-\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(t)
 \right]dt.}
\end{equation}
The integral is absolutely convergent and contains no $\kappa$.  Hence every
admissible path with the same two endpoint ordinate sets gives the same
smoothed endpoint difference.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $T>10$, Stieltjes integration gives
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{\gamma_k\le T}Q_\varphi(\gamma_k)
 -\sum_{\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}\le T}Q_\varphi(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})
 &=Q_\varphi(T)(N_\zeta-N_{\rm RVM})(T)\\
 &\quad-\int_{10}^{T}Q_\varphi'(t)
 (N_\zeta-N_{\rm RVM})(t)\,dt.
\tag{S17.15}
\end{align*}
Since
\begin{equation*}
 N_{\rm RVM}(t)
 =\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(t)
 -\mathfrak s(\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(t)+1/2),
\tag{S17.16}
\end{equation*}
one has
\begin{equation*}
 N_\zeta-N_{\rm RVM}
 =S+(\mathcal H_\zeta-\mathcal N_{\rm RVM})
 +\mathfrak s(\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}+1/2)=O(\log t).
\tag{S17.17}
\end{equation*}
Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-path-Ei-smoothing} gives
$Q_\varphi(T)=O(T^{-1})$ and $Q_\varphi'(t)=O(t^{-2})$, so the boundary
term tends to zero and the integral is absolutely convergent.  Matching the
finite-height sums with the index-paired difference costs only
$O(\log T)$ terms of size $O(T^{-1})$, hence tends to zero.  Finally
Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-path-distributional-J} supplies the factor $-2$ in
the endpoint series, which reverses the sign from the Stieltjes integration
and gives \CGeqref{eq:RVM-path-route-independent}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Crude localized bound for the smoothed endpoint difference]
\label{cor:RVM-path-smoothed-bound}
Let
$\varphi_\eta(u)=\eta^{-1}\rho((u-u_0)/\eta)$ with
$\rho\in C_c^1([-1,1])$, $\int\rho=1$, $x=e^{u_0}$ and
$0<\eta\le1$ with $\eta<u_0$, so that the test-function support lies in
$(0,\infty)$.  Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-smoothed-bound}
 |\langle J_{\rm RVM,1}^{\rm path}-J_{\rm RVM,0}^{\rm path},\varphi_\eta\rangle|
 \ll_\rho\sqrt{x}\left(
 1+\log^2\frac1\eta\right).
\end{equation}
In particular, as $x\to\infty$, $\eta\asymp(\log x)/x$ gives
$O(\sqrt{x}\log^2x)$.  Under $\mathbf P$ the same estimate applies to the
smoothed difference $J-J_{\rm RVM}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The Fourier integration by parts in
Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-path-Ei-smoothing} gives
\begin{equation*}
 |Q_{\varphi_\eta}'(t)|
 \ll_\rho\sqrt{x}\,\frac{\min(1,(\eta t)^{-1})}{t}.
\tag{S17.18}
\end{equation*}
Combine this with $|N_\zeta(t)-N_{\rm RVM}(t)|\ll\log t$ in the proof of
Proposition~\CGref{prop:RVM-path-route-independent}.  Splitting the integral
at $t=\max(10,1/\eta)$ gives
$O(1+\log^2(1/\eta))$ for the first part and
$O(1+\log(1/\eta))$ for the tail.  The constant term keeps the
bound valid throughout $0<\eta\le1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Formal stationary-phase expansion of the sawtooth term]
\label{prop:RVM-path-resonant-formal}
This proposition is a \emph{formal asymptotic calculation}, not a proved
uniform expansion.  If the Fourier series of the sawtooth in
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-path-route-independent} is truncated and stationary phase is
applied term by term away from a fixed lower cutoff $T_{\rm cut}\ge10$, the
resonant contribution is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-path-Zres}
 Z_{\rm res}(x)
 \sim\frac1\pi
 \sum_{\substack{m\ge1\\2\pi x^{1/m}>T_{\rm cut}}}
 (-1)^m m^{-3/2}x^{(m-1)/(2m)}
 \sin\!\left(2\pi m x^{1/m}+\frac{\pi(m-1)}4\right).
\end{equation}
The stationary point of the $m$th harmonic is
$t_m=2\pi x^{1/m}$.  The first term is
$-\pi^{-1}\sin(2\pi x)$.  Consequently its derivative has the same
frequency and magnitude $2\cos(2\pi x)$ as the first RVM Poisson harmonic,
with the opposite sign because
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-path-Zres} is written for $J_{\rm RVM,1}^{\rm path}-J_{\rm RVM,0}^{\rm path}$ rather than $J_{\rm RVM,0}^{\rm path}-J_{\rm RVM,1}^{\rm path}$.
The derivative of the $m=2$ term has size
$2^{-1/2}x^{-1/4}$, again with the corresponding endpoint-difference sign.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Only the phase calculation is asserted.  Use
\begin{equation*}
 \mathfrak s(y)=-\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{\sin(2\pi my)}{\pi m}
\tag{S17.19}
\end{equation*}
with a finite harmonic cutoff.  Since
$2\pi\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}'(t)=\log(t/2\pi)$, the two exponential phases are
$tu+2\pi m\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(t)$ and
$2\pi m\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(t)-tu$.  The first has no stationary point;
the second has
\begin{equation*}
 \Phi_m'(t)=m\log(t/2\pi)-u,
 \qquad
 t_m=2\pi x^{1/m},
 \qquad
 \Phi_m''(t_m)=m/t_m.
\tag{S17.20}
\end{equation*}
At the stationary point,
\begin{equation*}
 \Phi_m(t_m)=-2\pi m x^{1/m}+\frac{7\pi m}{4}.
\tag{S17.21}
\end{equation*}
The amplitude in $Q_\varphi'$ contributes
$\tfrac12\sqrt{2\pi/(mt_m)}$, and multiplication by the sawtooth coefficient
$(-1)^{m+1}/(\pi m)$ and the outer factor $2\sqrt{x}$ gives exactly the
coefficient and phase in \CGeqref{eq:RVM-path-Zres}.  A complete theorem
would still have to bound the nonstationary pieces, the stationary-phase
remainders uniformly in both $m$ and $x$, the harmonic tail, and the lower
cutoff terms.  Those estimates are not supplied here.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Status of the ordinate-homotopy chain]
\label{rem:RVM-path-status}
The status is deliberately separated.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{p{0.19\linewidth}p{0.70\linewidth}}
\toprule
Status & Content\\
\midrule
Unconditional theorem &
Theorems~\CGref{thm:RVM-critical-line-path},
\CGref{thm:RVM-path-distributional-J},
\CGref{thm:RVM-path-J0-uniform},
Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-path-J1-convergence}(a),
Proposition~\CGref{prop:RVM-path-route-independent} and
Corollary~\CGref{cor:RVM-path-smoothed-bound}.\\
Theorem under $\mathbf P$ &
Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-path-J1-convergence}(b) and
Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-path-endpoint-identity}.  By
Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-path-endpoint-identification}, $\mathbf P$ is exactly
RH, so these are not independent proofs of RH.\\
Formal calculation &
Proposition~\CGref{prop:RVM-path-resonant-formal}; the uniform remainder and
lower-cutoff analysis are open.\\
Open targets &
Pointwise convergence for $0<\kappa<1$ (not needed for the endpoint
identity), positivity of the sampled RVM weights, the Mellin identification
of the weighted Euler object, and the continuous-to-atomic endpoint problem
\CGeqref{eq:zeta-line-D}.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The next analytic target specific to the formal resonance calculation is a
uniform stationary-phase remainder in $m$ and $x$, followed by the same
analysis after the finite Möbius inversion defining $\pi_{\rm RVM}$.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Other deformations are logically separate]
Integer sampling of the RVM continuous count produces a weighted Euler
object but requires the unit-cell and Mellin-identification hypotheses.
Fractional interpolation between an RVM Euler measure and the true prime
measure can introduce branch monodromy at a genuine zero.  Neither issue is
needed for the critical-line ordinate path above, whose endpoint difference
is already determined by Proposition~\CGref{prop:RVM-path-route-independent}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The true-$\beta$ RVM ordinate transport}
\label{subsec:RVM-ordinate-transport}

The preceding critical system must be distinguished from the following
transported system.  Here the genuine real parts $\beta_k$ are retained and
only the ordinates are replaced by the deterministic RVM clock.  Thus this
object keeps the unknown horizontal zero geometry and is the one whose pole
abscissa remains $\Theta$.

Recall from Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-Hadamard-normalization} that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-true-canonical-product}
 P_\zeta(s)=2\xi(s)=G(s)\zeta(s)=P_\zeta^{\rm pair}(s).
\end{equation}
Define the true-$\beta$ rigid-ordinate product
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-rigid-canonical-product}
 P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)
 :=\prod_{k\ge1}
 \left(1-\frac{s}{\beta_k+i\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}}\right)
 \left(1-\frac{s}{\beta_k-i\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}}\right).
\end{equation}
$P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(0)=P_\zeta(0)=1$; the genuine paired product is exactly $P_\zeta$ by Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-Hadamard-normalization}.

\begin{lemma}[Absolute convergence of the paired rigid product]
\label{lem:RVM-rigid-product-convergence}
The product \CGeqref{eq:RVM-rigid-canonical-product} converges absolutely and
locally uniformly in $s$. It defines an entire function whose zeros, with
multiplicity, are exactly
$\beta_k\mathbin{\pm}i\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $\rho=\beta+i y$ with $0<\beta<1$,
\begin{equation}
 \left(1-\frac{s}{\rho}\right)
 \left(1-\frac{s}{\bar\rho}\right)
 =1-\frac{2\beta s}{\beta^2+y^2}
 +\frac{s^2}{\beta^2+y^2}
 =1+O_K(y^{-2})
\end{equation}
uniformly for $s$ in a compact set $K$.  The Riemann--von Mangoldt law for
the deterministic clock gives
$\sum_k(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^{-2}<\infty$.  The standard criterion for
infinite products proves absolute local uniform convergence.  The same
estimate shows that no additional zeros are introduced.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A multiplicative true-$\beta$ transport with no derivative of zeta}

For $s$ away from the displayed poles define
\begin{align}
\label{eq:RVM-log-transport}
 \mathscr L_{\rm RVM}(s)
 :=\sum_{k\ge1}\bigg(&
 \frac1{s-\beta_k-i\gamma_k}
 +\frac1{s-\beta_k+i\gamma_k}\notag\\
 &-\frac1{s-\beta_k-i\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}}
 -\frac1{s-\beta_k+i\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}}
 \bigg).
\end{align}
The zeros in \CGeqref{eq:RVM-true-zero-enumeration} occur with their
multiplicities, so no separate multiplicity factor is needed.

\begin{lemma}[Normal convergence of the logarithmic transport]
\label{lem:RVM-log-transport-convergence}
The series \CGeqref{eq:RVM-log-transport} converges normally on every compact
set avoiding its poles.  It therefore defines a meromorphic function.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $0<\beta<1$ put
\begin{equation}
 A(s;\beta,y)
 :=\frac1{s-\beta-iy}+\frac1{s-\beta+iy}
 =\frac{2(s-\beta)}{(s-\beta)^2+y^2}.
\end{equation}
Uniformly for $s$ in a fixed compact set and $0<\beta<1$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\partial}{\partial y}A(s;\beta,y)=O(y^{-3})
 \qquad(y\to\infty).
\end{equation}
Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-bounded-displacement} and the mean-value theorem make
the $k$th paired difference in \CGeqref{eq:RVM-log-transport}
$O(\gamma_k^{-3})$.  Finally the Riemann--von Mangoldt estimate implies
$\sum_k\gamma_k^{-3}<\infty$.  The Weierstrass test proves normal
convergence away from the finitely many poles meeting a given compact set.
\end{proof}

The residues of $\mathscr L_{\rm RVM}$ are integers: $+1$ at each genuine
zero occurrence and $-1$ at its transported occurrence. Hence the
exponential of a primitive is single-valued.

\begin{definition}[Transport multiplier and transported Mertens transform]
\label{def:RVM-transport-multiplier}
The Riemann--von Mangoldt transport multiplier is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-transport-multiplier}
 \mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)
 :=\frac{P_\zeta(s)}{P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)}.
\end{equation}
Its logarithmic derivative is exactly $\mathscr L_{\rm RVM}(s)$. Equivalently,
it is the exponential of a primitive of $\mathscr L_{\rm RVM}$, normalized
by $\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(0)=1$; integer residues make that exponential
single-valued. Define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Mvhat-definition}
 \boxed{
 \widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)
 :=\frac{G(s)}{P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)}
 =\frac{\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)}{\zeta(s)} .}
\end{equation}
Here the hat denotes the normalized Mellin--Perron transform, not a second
summatory function.
\end{definition}

Equivalently, the multiplier is the normally convergent product
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-transport-product}
 \mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)
 =\prod_{k\ge1}
 \frac{((s-\beta_k)^2+\gamma_k^2)/(\beta_k^2+\gamma_k^2)}
 {((s-\beta_k)^2+(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^2)/
  (\beta_k^2+(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^2)} .
\end{equation}
Indeed, logarithmic differentiation gives $\mathscr L_{\rm RVM}$; normal convergence follows by differentiating
each normalized logarithmic factor with respect to its ordinate and using
the proof of Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-log-transport-convergence}.

\begin{proposition}[Exact divisor transport]
\label{prop:RVM-exact-divisor-transport}
As divisors, with multiplicities and including possible coincidences,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-transport-divisor}
 \operatorname{div}(\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord})
 =\sum_{k\ge1}[\beta_k\mathbin{\pm}i\gamma_k]
 -\sum_{k\ge1}[\beta_k\mathbin{\pm}i\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}].
\end{equation}
Consequently all genuine nontrivial poles of $1/\zeta$ are cancelled in
$\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$, and its nontrivial pole divisor is exactly
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Mvhat-poles}
 \boxed{
 \sum_{k\ge1}[\beta_k\mathbin{\pm}i\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}].}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-pole-abscissa}
 \sup\{\Re s:s\text{ is a nontrivial pole of }\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}\}
 =\sup_k\beta_k=\Theta.
\end{equation}
No simplicity hypothesis and no value of $\zeta'(\rho_k)$ is used.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
At a pole of $\mathscr L_{\rm RVM}$ with residue $m\in\mathbb Z$, a local
primitive is $m\log(s-s_0)$ plus a holomorphic function.  Thus
$\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ has divisor order $m$ at $s_0$, proving
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-transport-divisor}.  The nontrivial pole divisor of
$1/\zeta$ is the negative of the genuine-zero divisor.  Divisors add under
multiplication, so \CGeqref{eq:RVM-Mvhat-definition} leaves precisely the
negative transported divisor, which is
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-Mvhat-poles}.  Vertical transport does not change any
$\beta_k$, and \CGeqref{eq:RVM-pole-abscissa} follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Functional symmetry of the rigid product]
\label{prop:RVM-rigid-functional-symmetry}
The product $P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ depends on the genuine zeros only through the ordered list
of real parts $(\beta_k)$ and their multiplicities; the true ordinates are
used only to assign that order. Moreover,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-rigid-functional-symmetry}
 \boxed{P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)=P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(1-s)\ \text{ for all }s
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \mathrm{RH}.}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If RH holds, every $\beta_k=1/2$, and each paired factor in
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-rigid-canonical-product} equals
\begin{equation}
 \frac{(s-1/2)^2+(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^2}
 {1/4+(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^2},
\end{equation}
which is invariant under $s\mapsto1-s$.

Conversely, suppose $P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)=P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(1-s)$.  Its zero divisor is then invariant
under $s\mapsto1-s$ and, because $P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ is real on the real axis, under
conjugation. Hence a zero
$\beta_k+i\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$ forces a zero
$1-\beta_k+i\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$. The sequence
$\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$ is strictly increasing, so the only upper-half-plane
zero of $P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ at that ordinate is the occurrence indexed by $k$. Therefore
$1-\beta_k=\beta_k$ for every $k$, proving RH.
\end{proof}

This symmetry is an exact reformulation, not an independent proof of RH.
Its possible value is that $P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ has a rigid and explicitly known ordinate
set; one would still need an argument, not already equivalent to RH, forcing
the symmetry or the required growth bound.

\subsection{An exact reflection defect}

The tie convention fixed above gives a second exact way to read horizontal
displacement from the rigid product.  Let $\mathcal J_{\rm off}$ index the
functional pairs away from the critical line.  For $j\in\mathcal J_{\rm off}$
write their real parts as $1/2+d_j$ and $1/2-d_j$, where
$0<d_j<1/2$.  By convention the right member is assigned the lower of the
two consecutive rigid ordinates.  Denote those ordinates by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-oriented-pair-ordinates}
 g_{j,0}^{\rm RVM}:=\gamma_{k_j}^{\rm RVM},
 \qquad
 g_{j,1}^{\rm RVM}:=\gamma_{k_j+1}^{\rm RVM},
 \qquad g_{j,0}^{\rm RVM}<g_{j,1}^{\rm RVM}.
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Positive reflection defect]
\label{prop:RVM-positive-reflection-defect}
For $x>0$ put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-reflection-defect-definition}
 \Delta_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(1/2+x)
 :=\log\frac{P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(1/2-x)}{P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(1/2+x)}.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-positive-reflection-defect}
 \boxed{
 \Delta_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(1/2+x)
 =8x\sum_{j\in\mathcal J_{\rm off}}d_j
 \int_{g_{j,0}^{\rm RVM}}^{g_{j,1}^{\rm RVM}}
 \frac{t\,dt}
 {\bigl(t^2+(x-d_j)^2\bigr)
  \bigl(t^2+(x+d_j)^2\bigr)}
 \ge0.}
\end{equation}
Equality for one, and hence for every, $x>0$ holds if and only if RH.
In particular,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Pv-one-criterion}
 \boxed{P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(1)\le1,
 \qquad P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(1)=1\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \mathrm{RH},}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-central-log-derivative}
 -\frac{\bigl(P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}\bigr)'(1/2)}{P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(1/2)}
 =2\sum_{j\in\mathcal J_{\rm off}}d_j
 \left(
 \frac1{d_j^2+(g_{j,0}^{\rm RVM})^2}
 -\frac1{d_j^2+(g_{j,1}^{\rm RVM})^2}
 \right)\ge0,
\end{equation}
with equality if and only if RH.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For real $r$, the paired conjugate factor belonging to a zero with real
part $b$ and positive ordinate $g$ is
\begin{equation}
 \left(1-\frac r{b+ig}\right)
 \left(1-\frac r{b-ig}\right)
 =\frac{(r-b)^2+g^2}{b^2+g^2}>0.
\end{equation}
Set
\begin{equation}
 F_{x,d}(g)
 :=\log\frac{g^2+(x+d)^2}{g^2+(x-d)^2}.
\end{equation}
The pair indexed by $j$ contributes
$F_{x,d_j}(g_{j,0}^{\rm RVM})-F_{x,d_j}(g_{j,1}^{\rm RVM})$ to
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-reflection-defect-definition}.  Direct differentiation gives
\begin{equation}
 -\frac{d}{dg}F_{x,d}(g)
 =\frac{8xdg}
 {\bigl(g^2+(x-d)^2\bigr)
  \bigl(g^2+(x+d)^2\bigr)}.
\end{equation}
Integration between $g_{j,0}^{\rm RVM}$ and $g_{j,1}^{\rm RVM}$ proves
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-positive-reflection-defect}.  Every summand is strictly
positive when $d_j>0$, while critical-line occurrences contribute zero.
Taking $x=1/2$ and using $P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(0)=1$ gives
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-Pv-one-criterion}.  Dividing
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-positive-reflection-defect} by $2x$ and letting $x\downarrow0$
gives \CGeqref{eq:RVM-central-log-derivative}; normal convergence justifies
the passage to the limit.
\end{proof}

The sign in Proposition~\CGref{prop:RVM-positive-reflection-defect} uses the
fixed within-pair ordering.  Reversing that ordering reverses the sign but
does not change the equality criterion.  Thus the vanishing is intrinsic to
the rigid divisor, whereas positivity records the chosen orientation of each
split functional pair.

\subsection{Horizontal zero energy encoded by the rigid product}

Put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-horizontal-displacement}
 \mathfrak d_k^{\rm RVM,ord}:=\beta_k-\frac12,
 \qquad
 I_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T):=\{k:T<\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}\le T+1\}.
\end{equation}
Define the horizontal energy and its level counts by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-horizontal-energy}
 E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T):=\sum_{k\in I_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T)}(\mathfrak d_k^{\rm RVM,ord})^2,
\end{equation}
and, for $0<a<1/2$, by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-horizontal-level-count}
 Q_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T)
 :=\#\{k\in I_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T):|\mathfrak d_k^{\rm RVM,ord}|>a\}.
\end{equation}
These quantities are not extra data attached to $P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$: they can be
recovered exactly from the logarithmic potential of the product.

\begin{proposition}[Riesz representation of the horizontal energy]
\label{prop:RVM-Riesz-horizontal-energy}
In the sense of distributions on the $s=\varsigma+it$ plane,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Riesz-measure}
 \boxed{
 \Delta_s\log|P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(\varsigma+it)|
 =2\pi\sum_{k\ge1}
 \left(
 \delta_{(\beta_k,\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})}
 +\delta_{(\beta_k,-\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})}
 \right),}
 \qquad
 \Delta_s:=\partial_{\varsigma}^2+\partial_t^2.
\end{equation}
Let $\psi\in C_c^\infty((0,\infty))$ and let
$\chi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$ equal $1$ on $[0,1]$. Then
\begin{align}
\label{eq:RVM-smoothed-horizontal-energy}
 \mathscr E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}[\psi]
 &:=\sum_{k\ge1}(\mathfrak d_k^{\rm RVM,ord})^2\psi(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})\notag\\
 &=\frac1{2\pi}\iint_{\mathbb R^2}
 \chi(\varsigma)\left(\varsigma-\frac12\right)^2\psi(t)
 \Delta_s\log|P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(\varsigma+it)|\,d\varsigma\,dt\notag\\
 &=\frac1{2\pi}\iint_{\mathbb R^2}
 \log|P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(\varsigma+it)|
 \Delta_s\!\left[
 \chi(\varsigma)\left(\varsigma-\frac12\right)^2\psi(t)
 \right]d\varsigma\,dt.
\end{align}
In particular $\mathscr E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}[\psi]\ge0$ when $\psi\ge0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For an entire function $F$, the distributional identity
$\Delta\log|F|=2\pi\sum_zm_z\delta_z$ counts its zeros with
multiplicity.  Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-rigid-product-convergence} identifies
the zero divisor of $P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$, proving \CGeqref{eq:RVM-Riesz-measure}.  Testing
that identity against
$\chi(\varsigma)(\varsigma-1/2)^2\psi(t)$ gives the second line of
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-smoothed-horizontal-energy}.  Distributional integration
by parts gives the third line.  All test functions have compact support, so
there are no boundary terms.
\end{proof}

Thus the two-dimensional surface $\log|P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}|$ determines the horizontal
energy without locating the zeros one at a time.  Equivalently, it is the
second moment, about the critical line, of the Riesz mass of that surface.

\begin{theorem}[Horizontal-energy criteria for RH and Lindel\"of]
\label{thm:RVM-horizontal-energy-criteria}
One has
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-horizontal-energy-RH}
 \boxed{
 \mathrm{RH}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T)=0\ \text{for every }T>0.}
\end{equation}
Moreover, the Lindel\"of hypothesis is equivalent to
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-horizontal-energy-Lindelof}
 \boxed{E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T)=o(\log T)\qquad(T\to\infty).}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The first assertion follows because every summand in
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-horizontal-energy} is nonnegative and the unit windows cover
all positive rigid ordinates.

For the second assertion, put $n_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T):=\#I_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T)$.  The defining relation
$\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})=k-1/2$ gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-local-clock-count}
 n_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T)
 =\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(T+1)-\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}(T)+O(1)
 =\frac1{2\pi}\log\frac{T}{2\pi}+O(1).
\end{equation}
For every $0<a<1/2$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-energy-level-bounds}
 \boxed{
 a^2Q_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T)
 \le E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T)
 \le a^2n_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T)+\frac14Q_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T).}
\end{equation}
The lower bound uses the terms with $|\mathfrak d_k^{\rm RVM,ord}|>a$.  For the upper bound, the
remaining terms contribute at most $a^2$ each and the exceptional terms at
most $1/4$ each.  Consequently
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-energy-level-equivalence}
 E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T)=o(\log T)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 Q_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T)=o(\log T)
 \quad\text{for every fixed }a>0.
\end{equation}
Indeed the forward implication follows from the lower bound.  Conversely,
first choose $a$ so small that the first term in the upper bound is an
arbitrarily small multiple of $\log T$, and then use the assumed estimate
for $Q_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T)$.

Backlund's local zero-density criterion states that the Lindel\"of
hypothesis is equivalent, for every fixed $a>0$, to
\begin{equation}
 N(1/2+a,T+1)-N(1/2+a,T)=o(\log T),
\end{equation}
where zeros are counted with multiplicity
\cite[Theorem~13.5]{Titchmarsh1986}.  Functional symmetry supplies the matching
count to the left of $1/2-a$.  Finally,
Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-bounded-displacement} says that true and rigid ordinates
differ by at most a fixed constant.  A unit window for either sequence is
therefore contained in a fixed-length window for the other sequence; that
window is covered by finitely many unit windows.  Hence the local
$o(\log T)$ property is unchanged by the RVM transport.  Combining this
observation with \CGeqref{eq:RVM-energy-level-equivalence} proves
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-horizontal-energy-Lindelof}.
\end{proof}

There is also the exact layer-cake identity
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-horizontal-energy-layer-cake}
 \boxed{
 E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T)=2\int_0^{1/2}a\,Q_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T)\,da.}
\end{equation}
Indeed $d^2=2\int_0^{1/2}a\mathbf1_{\{|d|>a\}}\,da$ for
$|d|<1/2$.  The count $Q_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T)$ itself is an argument-principle count of
the zeros of $P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ in the two rectangles outside
$|\Re s-1/2|\le a$.  Thus
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-horizontal-energy-layer-cake} is another relation involving
only the rigid product.

The distinction between the two criteria is essential.  Exact compatibility
with RH requires zero horizontal energy in every window.  The weaker bound
$E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T)=o(\log T)$ is exactly Lindel\"of and still permits infinitely many
off-line zeros.  Even $E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T)=O(1)$ would not force RH, since it could allow
a bounded number of horizontally displaced zeros in every window.

Define the global critical-line rigid reference by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-critical-reference-product}
 P_{\rm RVM}^{1/2}(s)
 :=\prod_{k\ge1}
 \frac{(s-1/2)^2+(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^2}
 {1/4+(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^2},
 \qquad
 \widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{1/2}(s):=\frac{G(s)}{P_{\rm RVM}^{1/2}(s)}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{The vertical resonant accumulation}

The horizontal energy also identifies precisely why a real-axis comparison
with the critical rigid reference does not automatically extend uniformly
up vertical lines.  For a fixed $a>0$ define the Poisson-smoothed resonant
energy
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-resonant-horizontal-energy}
 \mathcal R_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T)
 :=\sum_{k\ge1}
 \frac{(\mathfrak d_k^{\rm RVM,ord})^2}{a^2+(T-\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^2}.
\end{equation}
The conjugate ordinates give an additional nonresonant term with
$T+\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$ in place of
$T-\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}$; it is absorbed in the bounded remainders below.

\begin{lemma}[Local and Poisson-smoothed horizontal energy]
\label{lem:RVM-local-resonant-energy}
For every fixed $a>0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-local-resonant-equivalence}
 \boxed{
 \mathcal R_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T)=o(\log T)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T)=o(\log T).}
\end{equation}
Unconditionally,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-resonant-trivial-bound}
 \mathcal R_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T)=O_a(\log(2+T)).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $\gamma_k^{\rm RVM}\in(T,T+1]$, the kernel in
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-resonant-horizontal-energy} is at least
$(a^2+1)^{-1}$.  Hence
\begin{equation}
 E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T)\le(a^2+1)\mathcal R_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T),
\end{equation}
which proves one implication.

For the converse, partition the positive rigid ordinates into unit windows.
The contribution of the window at vertical distance between $m$ and $m+1$
from $T$ is at most a constant multiple of
\begin{equation}
 \frac{E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T+m)+E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(T-m-1)}{a^2+m^2}.
\end{equation}
If $E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(U)=o(\log U)$, the windows with $|m|\le T/2$ contribute
$o(\log T)$ because $\sum_{m\ge0}(a^2+m^2)^{-1}<\infty$.
The remaining windows contribute $o(\log T)$ by the trivial estimate
$E_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(U)\le n_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(U)/4=O(\log(2+U))$ and the square-summable kernel.  The same
partition with the trivial estimate in every window proves
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-resonant-trivial-bound}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Vertical comparison with the critical rigid reference]
\label{lem:RVM-vertical-reference-comparison}
For $s=\varsigma+iT$ with real $T\ge0$, $\varsigma\ge\Theta+a$ and
fixed real $a>0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-reference-ratio-bound}
 \boxed{
 \left|
 \log\left|
 \frac{\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(\varsigma+iT)}
 {\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{1/2}(\varsigma+iT)}
 \right|
 \right|
 \ll_a 1+\mathcal R_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T).}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For a paired conjugate factor put
\begin{equation}
 h_b(s,g)
 :=\log\left|
 \frac{(s-b)^2+g^2}{b^2+g^2}
 \right|.
\end{equation}
The contribution of an off-line functional pair to
$\log|P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}/P_{\rm RVM}^{1/2}|$ is
\begin{equation}
 h_{1/2+d_j}(s,g_{j,0}^{\rm RVM})-h_{1/2}(s,g_{j,0}^{\rm RVM})
 +h_{1/2-d_j}(s,g_{j,1}^{\rm RVM})-h_{1/2}(s,g_{j,1}^{\rm RVM}).
\end{equation}
Taylor's formula with integral remainder separates this expression into
\begin{equation}
 d_j\left(
 \partial_bh_b(s,g_{j,0}^{\rm RVM})-
 \partial_bh_b(s,g_{j,1}^{\rm RVM})
 \right)_{b=1/2}
\end{equation}
and a remainder bounded by $d_j^2$ times the supremum of
$|\partial_b^2h_b|$ along $|b-1/2|\le d_j$.  Because the intervals
$[g_{j,0}^{\rm RVM},g_{j,1}^{\rm RVM}]$ are disjoint, the total first-order contribution is at
most
\begin{equation}
 \frac12\int_0^\infty
 \left|\partial_g\partial_bh_b(s,g)\big|_{b=1/2}\right|\,dg
 \ll_a1.
\end{equation}
Here the last estimate follows by factoring
$(s-b)^2+g^2=(s-b-ig)(s-b+ig)$: each resulting Cauchy kernel has horizontal
distance at least $a$ from its singularity.

The same factorization gives, uniformly on the Taylor segment,
\begin{equation}
 |\partial_b^2h_b(s,g)|
 \ll_a
 \frac1{a^2+(T-g)^2}
 +\frac1{a^2+(T+g)^2}
 +\frac1{1+g^2}.
\end{equation}
After multiplication by $(\mathfrak d_k^{\rm RVM,ord})^2$ and summation, the first term is
$\mathcal R_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T)$, the second is bounded because it is nonresonant, and
the third is bounded because
$\sum_k(\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})^{-2}<\infty$.  Since
$\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}/\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{1/2}=P_{\rm RVM}^{1/2}/P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$, the same estimate holds with
the sign reversed and proves \CGeqref{eq:RVM-reference-ratio-bound}.
\end{proof}

The unconditional consequence of
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-reference-ratio-bound} is only polynomial comparison with
the critical reference.  To obtain a subpolynomial comparison from this
argument one needs
$\mathcal R_{\rm RVM,a}^{\rm ord}(T)=o(\log T)$.  By
Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-local-resonant-energy} and
Theorem~\CGref{thm:RVM-horizontal-energy-criteria}, that requirement is
equivalent to Lindel\"of.  Thus the moving resonant layer contains exactly
the obstruction missed by the bounded real-axis comparison; it cannot be
discarded merely because all rigid ordinates are explicit.

\subsection{Poisson geometry and the interpretation of rigid-product plots}

The geometry seen in numerical plots of $P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ is explained by a local
Poisson calculation.  Put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-local-density}
 \varrho_{\rm RVM}(T)
 :=\mathcal N_{\rm RVM}'(T)
 =\frac1{2\pi}\log\frac{T}{2\pi}.
\end{equation}
Freeze this density near a large height $T$ and replace the local clock by
an exact lattice of spacing $1/\varrho_{\rm RVM}(T)$.  If the real parts in
that local lattice all equal a fixed value $b$, the sine-product identity
gives, after absorbing the nonresonant conjugate factors and normalization
into a smooth term $B_{\rm RVM;\varsigma,b}(t)$,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:RVM-local-Poisson-wave}
 \log|P_{\rm RVM,b}^{\rm loc}(\varsigma+it)|
 &\simeq B_{\rm RVM;\varsigma,b}(t)
 +\frac12\log\!\left(
 2\cosh\bigl(2\pi\varrho_{\rm RVM}(T)|\varsigma-b|\bigr)
 -2\cos\vartheta(t)
 \right)\notag\\
 &=B_{\rm RVM;\varsigma,b}^{\rm sm}(t)
 -\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{
 e^{-2\pi m\varrho_{\rm RVM}(T)|\varsigma-b|}}
 {m}\cos\bigl(m\vartheta(t)\bigr),
\end{align}
where $\vartheta(t)$ is the unfolded clock phase.  This is a frozen-density
local model, not an exact global identity for the nonlinear RVM clock.  It
shows that the first harmonic has envelope
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-first-harmonic-envelope}
 \boxed{
 A_{\rm RVM,1}(T;\varsigma,b)
 \asymp
 e^{-2\pi\varrho_{\rm RVM}(T)|\varsigma-b|}
 =\left(\frac{T}{2\pi}\right)^{-|\varsigma-b|}.}
\end{equation}

For the global critical-line reference
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-critical-reference-product}, the Poisson calculation,
combined formally with the Stirling factor in
$G$, predicts for every fixed $\varsigma>1/2$ the two-sided reference bound
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-critical-reference-prediction}
 |\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{1/2}(\varsigma+iT)|\asymp_{\varsigma}1.
\end{equation}
This is recorded as the rigid-reference prediction.  A global proof with
uniform upper and lower constants is not supplied here, and none of the
proved criteria in this section uses
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-critical-reference-prediction} as an input.

This formula accounts for the two numerical surfaces associated with the
rigid product.  When $d=0$, every displayed zero has real part $1/2$,
$\Theta=1/2$, and the normalized surface is a regular corrugation in the
unfolded vertical coordinate whose amplitude decreases with the distance
from the critical line.  All base points lie on that line and
$P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)=P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(1-s)$.

When five functional pairs are displaced by $d=0.45$, their real parts are
$0.95$ and $0.05$, ten displayed zeros lie off the critical line, and
$\Theta=0.95$.  The finite displaced block changes the distance from a
fixed cut to the nearby zeros.  The previously regular crests acquire
different local heights, deep cusps appear near the displaced zeros, and the
modulation is confined to the affected portion of the clock.  Because the
two members of each functional pair occupy distinct consecutive RVM
ordinates, the transported divisor is no longer invariant under
$s\mapsto1-s$, exactly as Proposition~\CGref{prop:RVM-rigid-functional-symmetry}
predicts.

Two limitations prevent this picture from becoming an automatic proof of
RH.  First, a single modulus cut at $\varsigma=\varsigma_0$ sees to leading
order only $|\varsigma_0-\beta_k|$.  Reflection
$\beta_k\mapsto2\varsigma_0-\beta_k$ preserves that distance, so regularity
of one cut cannot determine on which side the zero lies.  Second, the
alternating configuration
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-alternating-counterexample}
 \beta_{2j-1}=\frac12+d,
 \qquad
 \beta_{2j}=\frac12-d
\end{equation}
has the same rigid clock and $\Theta=1/2+d$, but is periodic with a doubled
cell in the unfolded coordinate.  It can therefore be regular without all
real parts being equal.  The finite perturbation in the displayed plot is a
localized defect, not a slowly varying version of
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-alternating-counterexample}.

After removing the smooth carrier, the correctly normalized first-harmonic
diagnostic is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-normalized-cut-distance}
 \mathcal D_{\rm RVM;\varsigma_0}(T)
 :=-\frac{\log|A_{\rm RVM,1}(T;\varsigma_0)|}
 {2\pi\varrho_{\rm RVM}(T)}
 \simeq|\varsigma_0-\beta(T)|.
\end{equation}
One cut recovers only a distance.  Two distinct exact distances
$\ell^{\rm cut}_j:=|\varsigma_j-\beta|$, $\varsigma_1\ne\varsigma_2$, give the algebraic
identity
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-two-cut-beta-recovery}
 \boxed{
 \beta=
 \frac{\varsigma_2^2-\varsigma_1^2-(\ell^{\rm cut}_2)^2+(\ell^{\rm cut}_1)^2}
 {2(\varsigma_2-\varsigma_1)}.}
\end{equation}
If the measured diagnostics satisfy $\mathcal D_j=\ell^{\rm cut}_j+\eta_j$, then the
reconstructed value $\widetilde\beta$ obtained by replacing $\ell^{\rm cut}_j$ with
$\mathcal D_j$ obeys
\begin{equation}
 |\widetilde\beta-\beta|
 \le\frac{2\ell^{\rm cut}_1|\eta_1|+2\ell^{\rm cut}_2|\eta_2|+\eta_1^2+\eta_2^2}
 {2|\varsigma_2-\varsigma_1|}.
\end{equation}
Thus the two-cut diagnostic is stable only to the extent that the individual
cut errors $\eta_j$ are controlled; the symbol $\simeq$ is not used as an
exact reconstruction formula.
The full two-dimensional surface is stronger still: by
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-Riesz-measure}, its logarithmic singularities are located
exactly at $(\beta_k,\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})$.  Consequently the plots read the
horizontal displacements already encoded in a given $P_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$; by themselves
they do not supply an independent bound on those displacements.

\subsection{The exact umbilical identity with the Mertens function}

The Mertens function enters before any explicit formula over zeros.  For
$\Re s>1$, partial summation gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Mellin-M}
 \widehat M(s)
 :=s\int_1^\infty M(x)x^{-s-1}\,dx
 =\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{\mu(n)}{n^s}
 =\frac1{\zeta(s)}.
\end{equation}
Combining this identity with Definition~\CGref{def:RVM-transport-multiplier}
gives the central relation
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-umbilical-transform}
 \boxed{
 \widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)
 =\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)\widehat M(s)
 =\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)\,
 s\int_1^\infty M(x)x^{-s-1}\,dx .}
\end{equation}
Thus $M$ is not inferred from a similarity between graphs: it occurs
exactly as one factor of the transformed construction.

\begin{definition}[Unsmoothed transported object]
\label{def:RVM-unsmoothed-Mv}
Whenever the following inverse exists as an ordinary function, define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-unsmoothed-Mv}
 M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(x)
 :=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}
 \widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)\frac{x^s}{s}\,ds,
 \qquad c>1.
\end{equation}
Without an inversion theorem in a specified function or distribution space,
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-unsmoothed-Mv} denotes only a formal inverse-Mellin symbol.  This convention prevents a
formal residue expansion from being mistaken for a pointwise-defined
function.
\end{definition}

Assume that the inverse-Mellin distributions in question exist in a common
space and that their convolution is defined there (for example, via a
weighted Fourier multiplier realization after $x=e^y$).  Let
$\kappa_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ be the inverse multiplier kernel of
$\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$.  In the multiplicative convolution notation
\begin{equation}
 (f*_{\rm Mel}g)(x):=\int_0^\infty f(x/u)g(u)\,\frac{du}{u},
\end{equation}
the Mellin convolution theorem turns
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-umbilical-transform} into the distributional identity
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-umbilical-convolution}
 \boxed{M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}=\kappa_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}*_{\rm Mel}M.}
\end{equation}
If the inverse multiplier is admissible on the same space, then also
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-reverse-umbilical-convolution}
 \boxed{M=(\kappa_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord})^{[-1]}*_{\rm Mel}M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord},}
 \qquad
 \mathcal M((\kappa_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord})^{[-1]})(s)
 =\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)^{-1}.
\end{equation}
Equations \CGeqref{eq:RVM-umbilical-transform}--
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-reverse-umbilical-convolution} are the precise relation
between $M$ and $M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$.  The forward identity is exact in every common Mellin-distribution
realization satisfying the stated existence and convolution premises.  The reverse pointwise identity requires a multiplier theorem
and is not silently assumed.

The exact transform of their difference is equally simple:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-difference-transform}
 \widehat{(M-M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord})}(s)
 =\frac{1-\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)}{\zeta(s)}.
\end{equation}
At a genuine zero which is not a transported location,
$\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ vanishes and hence
$1-\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}=1$.  Therefore the corresponding pole of
$1/\zeta$ survives in \CGeqref{eq:RVM-difference-transform}.  This proves
that vertical displacements need not make $M-M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ smaller in exponent.
More precisely, if for every $\eta>0$ there is a genuine zero with
$\beta_k>\Theta-\eta$ which is not a transported location, then the pole
abscissa of \CGeqref{eq:RVM-difference-transform} is also $\Theta$.

\subsection{What the poles prove, and what they do not}

For an ordinary function $F$ with normalized Mellin transform, put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-growth-exponent}
 \vartheta(F):=\inf\bigl\{a:\
 F(x)=O_\varepsilon(x^{a+\varepsilon})
 \text{ for every }\varepsilon>0\bigr\}.
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[The unconditional pole obstruction]
\label{prop:RVM-pole-obstruction}
Assume that the ordinary function $M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ exists, that
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-Mvhat-definition} is its normalized Mellin transform in some
right half-plane, and that the usual Mellin continuation implication is
valid there.  Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-exponent-lower}
 \boxed{\vartheta(M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord})\ge\Theta.}
\end{equation}
Under the noncoincidence condition stated after
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-difference-transform}, the same argument gives
\begin{equation}
 \vartheta(M-M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord})\ge\Theta.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If $M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(x)=O_\varepsilon(x^{a+\varepsilon})$, its normalized Mellin
transform is holomorphic in $\Re s>a$.  Proposition~\CGref{prop:RVM-exact-divisor-transport}
exhibits poles with real parts arbitrarily close to $\Theta$, forcing
$a\ge\Theta$.  The difference follows from
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-difference-transform} in the same way.
\end{proof}

The reverse inequality $\vartheta(M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord})\le\Theta$ does not follow from the
locations of the poles.  It is a growth and inversion statement.  In
particular, the identity
\begin{equation}
 \vartheta(M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord})=\Theta
\end{equation}
must not be inserted between two equivalences until that upper bound has
been proved.  This distinction is exactly analogous to the distinction
between locating singularities of a Mellin transform and controlling its
inverse on vertical lines.

\subsection{A Gaussian-regularized criterion with explicit inversion}
\label{subsec:RVM-Gaussian-criterion}

The Gaussian regularization separates divisor location from unsmoothed
pointwise inversion, but the transform convention must be kept explicit.
The normalized unilateral transform used for Mertens-type functions is
\begin{equation}
 \widehat F(s):=s\int_1^\infty F(x)x^{-s-1}\,dx,
\end{equation}
whereas the bilateral Mellin transform used below is
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal M_{\rm bil}f(s):=\int_0^\infty f(x)x^{-s-1}\,dx.
\end{equation}
They are not interchangeable without the factor $s$ and without controlling
the part $0<x<1$.

The zero count $N_\zeta(T)=O(T\log T)$ and the bounded-displacement lemma
give the following standard finite-order estimate on any fixed vertical line
at positive distance from the zero and pole divisors: for every
$0<\delta<1$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-transport-finite-order}
 \log^+|\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(\sigma+it)|
 +\log^+|\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(\sigma+it)^{-1}|
 =O_{\sigma,\delta}((2+|t|)^{1+\delta}).
\end{equation}
The same type of bound is assumed/used for
$\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ on the closed zero-free strips through
which a contour is moved.  The restriction $\delta<1$ is what makes the
factor $e^{-t^2}$ dominate the vertical growth.

\begin{definition}[Gaussian-regularized transported baseline]
\label{def:RVM-Gaussian-Mv}
For $x>0$ and $c>1$, define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Gaussian-Mv}
 M_{\rm RVM,G}^{\rm ord}(x)
 :=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}
 e^{s^2}\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)\frac{x^s}{s}\,ds.
\end{equation}
The factor $1/s$ is part of the inversion of the normalized unilateral
transform.  On lines $c>1$ the integral is absolutely convergent.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Gaussian bilateral inversion]
\label{lem:RVM-Gaussian-inversion}
Let $H(s):=e^{s^2}\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)/s$.  Suppose that in a
vertical strip $0<a<b$ the function $H$ is holomorphic and satisfies the
pre-Gaussian finite-order bound
$|e^{-s^2}H(s)|\le\exp(C(1+|\Im s|)^{1+\delta})$, with
$0<\delta<1$, uniformly on every closed substrip.  Then the inverse
integral on any line $c\in(a,b)$ defines the same function $M_{\rm RVM,G}^{\rm ord}(x)$, the
horizontal sides of the displacement rectangles vanish, and whenever
\begin{equation*}
 M_{\rm RVM,G}^{\rm ord}(x)=O(x^{a+\eta})\quad(x\to\infty),\qquad
 M_{\rm RVM,G}^{\rm ord}(x)=O(x^{b-\eta})\quad(x\downarrow0)
\tag{S17.22}
\end{equation*}
for some $\eta>0$, one has
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Gaussian-bilateral-transform}
 \mathcal M_{\rm bil}M_{\rm RVM,G}^{\rm ord}(s)=H(s)
\end{equation}
throughout $a+\eta<\Re s<b-\eta$, when this strip is nonempty.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
On a vertical line, $|e^{(\sigma+it)^2}|=e^{\sigma^2-t^2}$, which dominates
$\exp(O(|t|^{1+\delta}))$ because $0<\delta<1$.  The same domination makes
the horizontal sides of a rectangular contour tend to zero and proves line
independence whenever no pole is crossed.  The two endpoint bounds make the
bilateral Mellin integral absolutely convergent in the stated strip.
On a common vertical line, put $x=e^y$.  The inverse is the Fourier
inverse of the integrable Gaussian-decaying function $H(c+it)$, and the
endpoint bounds make $e^{-cy}M_{\rm RVM,G}^{\rm ord}(e^y)$ integrable.  Fourier inversion and
uniqueness therefore identify its Fourier transform with $H(c+it)$;
holomorphy extends the equality through the common strip.  This avoids an
unjustified absolute interchange over the entire multiplicative group.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Gaussian transported-divisor criterion]
\label{thm:RVM-Gaussian-RH}
Assume the vertical finite-order bounds required in
Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-Gaussian-inversion}.  Then the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item RH;
\item for every $\varepsilon>0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-Gaussian-RH-bound}
 M_{\rm RVM,G}^{\rm ord}(x)=O_\varepsilon(x^{1/2+\varepsilon})
 \qquad(x\to\infty).
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
This criterion uses the genuine multiplicities and the transported pairs
$(\beta_k,\gamma_k^{\rm RVM})$ and is therefore a Category~3 divisor
criterion, not an independent construction of the real parts $\beta_k$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Assume RH and fix $\varepsilon>0$.  All nontrivial poles of
$\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ lie on $\Re s=1/2$.  Shift
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-Gaussian-Mv} from $c>1$ to
$\Re s=1/2+\varepsilon$.  No nontrivial pole is crossed.  The point $s=1$
is a zero, not a pole, of $1/\zeta$ in the factorization
$\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}=\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}/\zeta$;
the trivial poles coming from negative even zeta zeros and the point $s=0$
lie to the left of the shifted line.  Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-Gaussian-inversion}
therefore justifies the displacement and gives
$O_\varepsilon(x^{1/2+\varepsilon})$.

Conversely assume the displayed bound for every $\varepsilon>0$.  Moving the
original line to any fixed $A>1$ inside the pole-free half-plane
$\Re s>1$ gives, by the same Gaussian estimate,
$M_{\rm RVM,G}^{\rm ord}(x)=O_A(x^A)$ as $x\downarrow0$.  Hence for each
$\varepsilon>0$ and $A>1$ the bilateral transform
\begin{equation*}
 \int_0^\infty M_{\rm RVM,G}^{\rm ord}(x)x^{-s-1}\,dx
\tag{S17.23}
\end{equation*}
is holomorphic in $1/2+\varepsilon<\Re s<A$.  By
Lemma~\CGref{lem:RVM-Gaussian-inversion} it equals
$e^{s^2}\widehat M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(s)/s$ first in the common strip
$1<\Re s<A$ and hence throughout by analytic continuation.  Because
$e^{s^2}/s$ is nonzero in $\Re s>1/2$, the transported transform has no
nontrivial pole there.  Proposition~\CGref{prop:RVM-exact-divisor-transport} then gives
$\beta_k\le1/2$ for every genuine zero.  Functional symmetry sends
$\beta_k$ to $1-\beta_k$, forcing $\beta_k=1/2$ and RH.
\end{proof}

The theorem is deliberately regularized.  Removing the Gaussian requires
unsmoothed vertical control and a pointwise Mellin multiplier theorem; neither
follows from the location of the transported poles.

\subsection{The unsmoothed transfer theorem that would complete the route}

The relation with $M$ becomes pointwise if the transport and its inverse
act boundedly on a suitable weighted Mellin space.  The following statement
records a sufficient hypothesis rather than concealing it in the notation.

\begin{hypothesis}[Bidirectional unsmoothed Mellin transfer]
\label{hyp:RVM-unsmoothed-transfer}
For every $\varepsilon>0$, assume that the inverse multiplier kernels
exist on a specified common Mellin space.  The distributions with Mellin transforms
$\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}$ and $(\mathscr T_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord})^{-1}$ act boundedly by
multiplicative convolution on the weighted supremum space
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal B_{\varepsilon}
 :=\{f:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb C:\ f(x)=0\ (0<x<1),
 \sup_{x\ge1}x^{-1/2-\varepsilon}|f(x)|<\infty\},
\end{equation}
and the distributional identities
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-umbilical-convolution} and
\CGeqref{eq:RVM-reverse-umbilical-convolution} hold pointwise there.
\end{hypothesis}

A stronger, concrete sufficient condition is that the two inverse-Mellin
kernels are signed measures whose weighted total variations are finite on
the multiplicative group.  Under
Hypothesis~\CGref{hyp:RVM-unsmoothed-transfer},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-pointwise-two-way-transfer}
 \boxed{
 M(x)=O_\varepsilon(x^{1/2+\varepsilon})
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(x)=O_\varepsilon(x^{1/2+\varepsilon}).}
\end{equation}
Together with Littlewood's criterion, this would give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:RVM-full-unsmoothed-chain}
 \boxed{
 \mathrm{RH}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \Theta=\frac12
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 M_{\rm RVM}^{\rm ord}(x)=O_\varepsilon(x^{1/2+\varepsilon})
 \text{ for every }\varepsilon>0.}
\end{equation}
The proof of \CGeqref{eq:RVM-pointwise-two-way-transfer} is immediate from
